acceptance (when the hierarchy is applied to AGC) as the problem of checking for the existence of some integer tensors satisfying certain geometric properties. This “ultra-processed” acceptance criterion will allow turning the quest for a fooling instance for
\BA^{k}
(the goal of this paper) into the problem of building certain special hollow-shadowed crystal tensors—which will be accomplished in later sections.

4.1
Relaxations and linear minions

All relaxation algorithms studied in the literature on CSPs and their promise variant are captured algebraically through the notion of linear minion, which we describe in this section.

Given two integers
\ell,m\in\mathbb{N}
and a function
\pi:[\ell]\to[m]
, let
P_{\pi}
be the
m\times\ell
matrix such that, for
i\in[m]
and
j\in[\ell]
, the
(i,j)
-th entry of
P_{\pi}
is
1
if
\pi(j)=i
, and
0
otherwise.

Definition 22

(
[
40
]
)
.

A
linear minion

{\mathscr{M}}
of
depth

{d}\in\mathbb{N}
consists of the union of sets
{\mathscr{M}}^{(\ell)}
of
\ell\times d
rational matrices
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, that satisfy the following condition:
P_{\pi}M\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(m)}
whenever
\ell,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\pi:[\ell]\to[m]
, and
M\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(\ell)}
.
8
8

8

The definition of linear minions we give here is less general than the one in
[
40
, Definition 16]
, which includes linear minions of infinite depth and whose matrices have entries in arbitrary semirings rather than
\mathbb{Q}
.

Observe that pre-multiplying a matrix
M
by
P_{\pi}
amounts to performing a combination of the following three elementary operations to the rows of
M
: swapping two rows, replacing two rows with their sum, and inserting a zero row. Hence, a linear minion is simply a set of matrices having a fixed number of columns that is closed under such elementary operations.

Example 23
.

For each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let

•

{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}^{(\ell)}
be the set of rational vectors of length
\ell
whose entries are nonnegative and sum up to
1
,

•

{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}^{(\ell)}
be the set of integer vectors of length
\ell
whose (possibly negative) entries sum up to
1
, and

•

{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}^{(\ell)}
be the set of
\ell\times 2
matrices whose left column
\mathbf{v}
belongs to
{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}^{(\ell)}
, whose right column
\mathbf{w}
belongs to
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}^{(\ell)}
, and such that, for each
i\in[\ell]
,
v_{i}=0
implies
w_{i}=0
.

Using that
\mathbf{1}_{m}^{T}P_{\pi}=\mathbf{1}_{\ell}^{T}
for each
\pi:[\ell]\to[m]
, we easily check that
{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}^{(\ell)}
and
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}^{(\ell)}
are both linear minions of depth
1
, while
{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}^{(\ell)}
is a linear minion of depth
2
.

In order to be consistent with the notation of
[
20
, Definition 5]
,
given a linear minion
{\mathscr{M}}
, a function
\pi:[\ell]\to[m]
, and a matrix
M\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(\ell)}
,
we shall often denote the product
P_{\pi}M
by the notation
M_{/\pi}
.

Remark 24
.

For two maps
\pi:[\ell]\to[m]
and
\sigma:[m]\to[p]
, we easily check that
P_{\sigma\circ\pi}=P_{\sigma}P_{\pi}
. As a consequence,

\displaystyle M_{/\sigma\circ\pi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(M_{/\pi})_{/\sigma}.

(12)

Also, if
\operatorname{id}
is the identity function on
[\ell]
,

P_{\operatorname{id}}
is the identity matrix of size
\ell\times\ell
, so
M_{/\operatorname{id}}=M
. This shows that linear minions form a subclass of the so-called
abstract minions
(or simply
minions
) introduced in
[
20
]
(see also
[
8
]
).

Each linear minion corresponds to a relaxation for (P)CSPs through the notion of free structure. Intuitively, the free structure of a linear minion
{\mathscr{M}}
generated by a hypergraph
\mathbf{H}
simulates the structure of
\mathbf{H}
inside
{\mathscr{M}}
: The vertices become matrices of
{\mathscr{M}}
, while the hyperedges are tuples of matrices that can all be obtained from a single other matrix through elementary row operations.
The formal definition
is given below. We define the free structure for uniform hypergraphs rather than digraphs, because we will later use it in that more general case. In fact, the same construction can be applied to arbitrary relational structures, see
[
8
, Definition 4.1]
.

Definition 25

(
[
8
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a
p
-uniform hypergraph having
n
vertices and
m
hyperedges. Without loss of generality, let the domain of
\mathbf{H}
be
[n]
.
The
free structure

\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{H})
of a linear minion
{\mathscr{M}}
generated by
\mathbf{H}
is the (potentially infinite)
p
-uniform hypergraph on the vertex set
V(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{H}))={\mathscr{M}}^{(n)}
whose hyperedges are defined as follows: Given
M_{1},\dots,M_{p}\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(n)}
, the tuple
(M_{1},\dots,M_{p})
belongs to
E(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{H}))
if and only if there exists some
Q\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(m)}
such that
M_{i}=Q_{/\pi_{i}}
for each
i\in[p]
, where
\pi_{i}:E(\mathbf{H})\to V(\mathbf{H})
maps a hyperedge
\mathbf{h}
to its
i
-th entry
h_{i}
.

Take a linear minion
{\mathscr{M}}
and two digraphs
\mathbf{X}
(the instance) and
\mathbf{A}
(the template). The relaxation corresponding to
{\mathscr{M}}
outputs
Yes
if
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A})
and
No
otherwise.
9
9

9

In
[
40
]
, this relaxation was described as the “minion test” associated with
{\mathscr{M}}
.

For certain linear minions, the problem of deciding whether
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A})
can be solved in polynomial time (in the size of the input
\mathbf{X}
) for any
\mathbf{A}
. In particular, this is the case for the linear minions
{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}
,
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
, and
{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}
from Example
23
. It was shown in
[
8
]
that
{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}
and
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
correspond to the polynomial-time relaxations
\BLP
and
\AIP
, respectively, while it was shown in
[
20
]
that
{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}
corresponds to the polynomial-time relaxation
\BA
.

In
[
40
]
, a class of linear minions enjoying particularly desirable features was identified.

Definition 26

(
[
40
]
)
.

A
conic minion

{\mathscr{M}}
is a linear minion of depth
d
such that
(i)

{\mathscr{M}}
does not contain any all-zero matrix, and
(ii)
for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, every
M\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(\ell)}
,
and every
V\subseteq[\ell]
, the following implication is true:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\sum_{i\in V}E_{i}\ast M=\mathbf{0}_{d}\quad\Rightarrow\quad E_{i}\ast M=\mathbf{0}_{d}\;\;\forall i\in V.\end{array}

In other words, a linear minion
{\mathscr{M}}
is conic if it does not contain all-zero matrices and if summing up nonzero rows of a matrix in
{\mathscr{M}}
does not yield the all-zero vector.

Example 27
.

It is not hard to check that
{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}
and
{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}
are conic, while
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
is not (cf.
[
40
]
).

The following property of the entries of
P_{\pi}
is a reformulation of
[
40
, Lemma 30]
and shall prove useful on multiple occasions. We include the simple proof for completeness.

Lemma 28
.

Let
\ell,m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\pi:[\ell]\to[m]
, and let
i\in[m]
. Then
E_{i}\ast P_{\pi}=\sum_{j\in\pi^{-1}(i)}E_{j}
.

Proof.

For any
z\in[\ell]
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\pi^{-1}(i)}E_{j}\ast E_{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{ if }z\in\pi^{-1}(i)\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{ if }\pi(z)=i\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{i}\ast P_{\pi}\ast E_{z},

which means that
\sum_{j\in\pi^{-1}(i)}E_{j}=E_{i}\ast P_{\pi}
, as required.
∎

4.2
Hierarchies and tensors

The framework developed in
[
40
]
allows to progressively strengthen the relaxation corresponding to any linear minion (called “minion test” therein) through the notion of tensor power of a digraph (given in
[
40
, Definition 10]
for the more general case of relational structures).

Definition 29

(
[
40
]
)
.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th tensor power
of a digraph
\mathbf{A}
is the
2^{k}
-uniform hypergraph
\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
having vertex set
V(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})=V(\mathbf{A})^{k}
and hyperedge set
E(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})=\{\mathbf{a}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}:\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{A})\}
where, for
\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{A})
,
\mathbf{a}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
is the tensor
in
\mathcal{T}^{2\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(V(\mathbf{A})^{k})
whose
\mathbf{i}
-th entry is
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
for every
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
.

Let us see what happens when we take the free structure generated by the tensor power of a digraph.

Remark 30
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a linear minion of depth
d
and let
\mathbf{A}
be a digraph with
n
vertices
10
10

10

Here and throughout the rest of the paper, we shall often assume that the vertex set of the digraph
\mathbf{A}
is
[n]
.
and
m
edges. Just like
\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
,
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is a
2^{k}
-uniform hypergraph. Its vertex set is
V(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))={\mathscr{M}}^{(n^{k})}
. Hence, the vertices of
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
are
n^{k}\times d
rational matrices; it will be convenient to identify them with tensors in
\mathcal{T}^{(n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k},d)}(\mathbb{Q})
. A family
\{M^{(\mathbf{i})}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}}
of vertices (i.e., of tensors in
V(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))
) forms a hyperedge if and only if there exists some matrix
Q\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(m)}
such that
M^{(\mathbf{i})}=Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
, where
\pi_{\mathbf{i}}:E(\mathbf{A})\to V(\mathbf{A})^{k}
maps
\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{A})
to
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Note that
Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
can be expressed as a contraction by the multilinear version of the matrix
P_{\pi_{i}}
associated with the map
\pi_{i}
from Definition
25
;
i.e.,
Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\overset{\tiny 1}{\ast}Q
, where
P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\in\mathcal{T}^{(n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k},m)}(\mathbb{Q})
is the tensor whose
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
-th entry is
1
if
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}
and
0
otherwise, for
\mathbf{a}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{k}
and
\mathbf{b}\in E(\mathbf{A})
.

The strategy introduced in
[
40
]
for strengthening a minion test consists in applying the test to the tensor powers of both the instance and the template—with one extra technicality: The homomorphism certifying acceptance of the relaxation thus obtained should be compatible with the tensorised structures, in the sense of Definition
31
.

Definition 31
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a linear minion, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two digraphs. We say that a homomorphism
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is
k
-tensorial
if
\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{x})
for any
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
,
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
.

In other words, a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
translates the operation of tuple projection into the operation of tensor projection—where the latter is expressed as contraction by the projection tensor
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}
introduced in Section
3.5
.

Given a linear minion
{\mathscr{M}}
and an integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th level of the relaxation induced by
{\mathscr{M}}
is defined as follows: For any pair of digraphs
\mathbf{X}
(the instance) and
\mathbf{A}
(the template), it outputs
Yes
if there exists a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
and
No
otherwise.
It was shown in
[
40
]
that both the
\BLP
and the
\AIP
hierarchies fit into this framework, in the sense that, for any two digraphs
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
and any integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\BLP^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})=\textsc{Yes}
(
\AIP^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})=\textsc{Yes}
) if and only if there exists a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
(
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
).
A similar characterisation was also established for the
\BA
hierarchy we consider in this work (see
[
40
, Theorem 15]
). Moreover, using that the minion
{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}
capturing the
\BA
hierarchy is the
semi-direct product
of the two minions
{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}
and
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
, it was shown in
[
40
, Proposition 44]
that any
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
can be split into homomorphisms to the free structures of
{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}
and
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
, separately. These results are summarised in the next theorem.

Theorem 32

(
[
40
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{A}
be digraphs and let
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
. The following are equivalent:

•

\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})=\textsc{Yes}
;

•

there exists a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
;

•

there exist
k
-tensorial homomorphisms
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
and
\zeta:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
such that
\supp(\zeta(\mathbf{x}))\subseteq\supp(\xi(\mathbf{x}))
for any
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
.

Remark 33
.

It was shown in
[
40
, Proposition 36]
that the existence of a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is equivalent to the existence of a homomorphism from
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}
are obtained from
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{A}
by
k
-enhancing
them, i.e., by adding to their signatures an extra relation that includes all tuples of length
k
. We prefer to adopt the description in terms of
k
-tensorial homomorphisms, as
k
-enhancing a digraph results in a structure having two different relations, while in this work we only consider structures with one relation (digraphs or hypergraphs). We also remark that the term “
k
-tensorial” does not appear in
[
40
]
.

4.3
BA
k
acceptance for AGC

The goal of this work is to show that no level of the
\BA
hierarchy solves the approximate graph colouring problem
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
. To that end, we need to find instances
\mathbf{X}
that are able to fool the hierarchy, i.e., such that
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{c})=\textsc{Yes}
but
\mathbf{X}
is not
d
-colourable.
It turns out that, for the particular case that the
\BA
hierarchy is applied to the colouring problem (i.e., when
\mathbf{A}
is a clique), the acceptance criterion of Theorem
32
can be simplified: As stated in Theorem
2
, it is enough to check for the existence of a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\zeta
from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
that satisfies a simple combinatorial condition. The reason why one does not have to explicitly verify the existence of a homomorphism
\xi
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
, too, is that, when the size of the clique
\mathbf{A}
is at least
k
, there exists a standard
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\xi_{0}
from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
that gives equal weight to
all
admitted assignments—equivalently, the tensors that are images of elements of
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
under
\xi_{0}
are uniform within their admitted support. This homomorphism is “as good as possible” for our purposes, in the sense that it makes the support of
\xi_{0}(\mathbf{x})
as large as it can be, thus leaving more room for the existence of some
\zeta
satisfying the refinement condition
\supp(\zeta(\mathbf{x}))\subseteq\supp(\xi(\mathbf{x}))
. In other words, whenever a pair of
k
-tensorial homomorphisms
(\xi,\zeta)
certifying
\BA^{k}
acceptance exists, the pair
(\xi_{0},\zeta)
also works. As it will later become more clear, thanks to the criterion given in Theorem
2
, we can view
\BA^{k}
acceptance in terms of the existence of a family of integer tensors satisfying a system of symmetries (dictated by the fact that
\zeta
needs to be a
k
-tensorial homomorphism) together with a “hollowness requirement” expressed through the extra combinatorial condition. The hollow-shadowed crystals we shall seek in the next section will generate a family of such tensors.

The proof of Theorem
2
makes use of two technical lemmas that we present next. The first is a special case of
[
40
, Lemma 32]
.
Recall the definition of the symbols “
\prec
” and “
\sim
” given in Section
3.2
.

Lemma 34

(
[
40
]
)
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a linear minion of depth
d
, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two digraphs, and let
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
be a
k
-tensorial homomorphism. Then
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{0}_{d}
for any
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{k}
for which
\mathbf{x}\not\prec\mathbf{a}
.

Crucially, Lemma
34
does not require that the linear minion be conic. In the proof of Theorem
2
, we shall apply this lemma to the (non-conic) minion
{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}
.

Lemma 35
.

Let
k\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X
be a set, and consider the tuples
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
,
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
, and
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
. Then

\displaystyle|\{\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}:\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\mbox{ and }\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}\}|=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|)!}{(n-|\mathbf{x}|)!}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Assume first that
\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Note that there exists a bijection
\vartheta
between the set
\{\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}:\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}\}

and the set of injective functions from
\set(\mathbf{x})
to
[n]
.
(Indeed,
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}
means that
b_{p}=b_{q}
if and only if
x_{p}=x_{q}
for every

p,q\in[k]
.) Now, if
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
, the restriction of
\vartheta(\mathbf{b})
to
\set(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})
is entirely determined by
\mathbf{a}
. The remaining values of

\vartheta(\mathbf{b})
can be chosen in

\displaystyle(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|)\cdot(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|-1)\cdots(n-|\mathbf{x}|+1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|)!}{(n-|\mathbf{x}|)!}

distinct ways, thus proving the first case in the statement of the lemma.

Assume now that
\mathbf{a}\not\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
. By definition, if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}
, then
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Thus, if
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}
, then
\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
, a contradiction. This proves the second case in the statement of the lemma.
∎

Theorem

(Theorem
2
restated)
.

Let
2\leq k\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X}
be a loopless digraph, and let
\zeta:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
be a
k
-tensorial homomorphism such that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})=0
for any
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
for which
\mathbf{a}\not\prec\mathbf{x}
.
Then
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{n})=\textsc{Yes}
.

Proof.

For
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
, consider the tensor
T_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Q})
defined by

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}.

We shall prove that the function

\displaystyle\xi:V(\mathbf{X})^{k}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}}T_{\mathbf{x}}

yields a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. First, observe that
\xi
is well defined as, using that
k\leq n
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{Pi_epsilon_all_one}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}|\{\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}:\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}\}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{n!}{(n-|\mathbf{x}|)!}

(13)

which is not zero.
Moreover, we have that
\xi(\mathbf{x})\in{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}^{(n^{k})}
since

\displaystyle\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\bm{\epsilon}}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}}{\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}1.

We now prove that
\xi
sends hyperedges of
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to hyperedges of
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Take
(x,y)\in E(\mathbf{X})
, so
(x,y)^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in E(\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
; since
\mathbf{X}
is loopless,
x\neq y
. Observe that
|E(\mathbf{K}_{n})|=n^{2}-n
. Take
Q=\frac{1}{n^{2}-n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n^{2}-n}\in{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}^{(n^{2}-n)}
; we claim that
\xi((x,y)_{\mathbf{i}})=Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
, which then implies that
\xi((x,y)^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in E(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))
, as needed. For
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{n^{2}-n}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast\mathbf{1}_{n^{2}-n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{n^{2}-n}\sum_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n})}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast E_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{n^{2}-n}|\{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n}):(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\}|.

(14)

Suppose that
\mathbf{i}=\mathbf{1}_{k}
. In this case, (
4.3
) yields

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{n^{2}-n}|\{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n}):(a^{\prime},\dots,a^{\prime})=\mathbf{a}\}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{n}&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\mbox{ is constant}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi((x,y)_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi((x,\dots,x))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{(x,\dots,x)}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{(x,\dots,x)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1255_28sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{(n-1)!}{n!}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{(x,\dots,x)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{n}&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\mbox{ is constant}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Hence, the claim holds in this case. The case
\mathbf{i}=2\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}
follows analogously. Suppose now that
|\mathbf{i}|=2
. In this case, (
4.3
) yields

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{n^{2}-n}&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{i}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi((x,y)_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1255_28sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{(n-2)!}{n!}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{n^{2}-n}&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\sim(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Using that
(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}\sim\mathbf{i}
and that “
\sim
” is transitive, we conclude that the claim holds in this case, too.
It follows that
\xi
is a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.
To show that
\xi
is
k
-tensorial, consider three tuples
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
,
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
, and
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
, and observe that

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1255_28sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}|)!}{n!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}|)!}{n!}|\{\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}:\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\mbox{ and }\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}\}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_involved_1511_1718}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}|)!}{n!}\cdot\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|)!}{(n-|\mathbf{x}|)!}&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|)!}{n!}&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{1}{\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1255_28sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|)!}{n!}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(n-|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}|)!}{n!}&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

It follows that
\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})
, which means that
\xi
is
k
-tensorial.

Take
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
, and suppose that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=0
. This implies
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast T_{\mathbf{x}}=0
, which means that
\mathbf{a}\not\sim\mathbf{x}
; i.e., either
\mathbf{a}\not\prec\mathbf{x}
or
\mathbf{x}\not\prec\mathbf{a}
. Using the hypothesis of the theorem (in the former case) or Lemma
34
applied to
\zeta
(in the latter case), we find that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})=0
.
It follows that
\supp(\zeta(\mathbf{x}))\subseteq\supp(\xi(\mathbf{x}))
for any
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
. By virtue of Theorem
32
, this
implies that
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{n})=\textsc{Yes}
.
∎

5
Crystals

In Section
4
, we obtained a multilinear criterion for the acceptance of the
\BA
hierarchy applied to AGC: According to Theorem
2
, to have
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{n})=\textsc{Yes}
it suffices to find a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\zeta
from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
satisfying the extra condition

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}\not\prec\mathbf{x}\;\;\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\;\;E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})=0.

(15)

(Note that, by virtue of Lemma
34
, the condition “
\mathbf{a}\not\prec\mathbf{x}
” might be replaced with “
\mathbf{a}\not\sim\mathbf{x}
”.)
It follows from Remark
30
that
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is a
2^{k}
-uniform infinite hypergraph whose vertices are elements of
\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
, i.e.,
k
-dimensional integer cubical tensors of width
n
, whose entries sum up to
1
. As for the hyperedges, a family
\{T^{(\mathbf{i})}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}}
of
2^{k}
such tensors forms a hyperedge if and only if there exists an integer vector
\mathbf{q}
of length
n^{2}-n=|E(\mathbf{K}_{n})|
(i.e., an integer distribution over the edges of
\mathbf{K}_{n}
) whose entries sum up to
1
and such that all tensors in the family can be obtained from
\mathbf{q}
by specific contractions; more precisely, we require that
T^{(\mathbf{i})}=\mathbf{q}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast\mathbf{q}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
.

Definition 36
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
, and let
T\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbb{Z})
. We say that
T
is
affine
if
\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast T=1
.

Hence, finding a homomorphism
\zeta
from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
means selecting some
k
-dimensional integer affine cubical tensors of width
n
(one for each tuple
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
) in such a way that the hyperedge relation is preserved. In order for
\zeta
to be
k
-tensorial, this family of tensors needs to behave well with respect to projections: The tensor associated with the (combinatorial) projection of a tuple
\mathbf{x}
of vertices onto a tuple
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
should be the (geometric) projection of the tensor associated with
\mathbf{x}
onto the hyperplane generated by
\mathbf{i}
; in symbols,
\zeta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})
.
One way to build a family of tensors having this property is to consider the
k
-dimensional projections of a single higher-dimensional affine cubical tensor
C
of width
n
, whose dimension
q
is the number of vertices of
\mathbf{X}
. Specifically,
we build a map
\zeta_{C}
associated with the tensor
C
as follows: The image of a tuple
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
under
\zeta_{C}
is the projection of
C
onto the hyperplane generated by
\mathbf{x}
; i.e., the tensor
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}}\ast C
. In this way,
\zeta_{C}
is automatically
k
-tensorial. Indeed, Lemma
20
and Lemma
16
imply that

\zeta_{C}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast C=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}}\ast C=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\zeta_{C}(\mathbf{x}),

as needed.

For the map
\zeta_{C}
to yield a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
, it is enough to require that the
2
-dimensional projections of
C
be equal up to taking the transpose and have zero diagonal (cf. the proof of Theorem
6
in Section
6
). Since a cubical tensor
C
of width
n
and dimension
q
with this property exists for all choices of
n\geq 3
and
q
,
any
loopless digraph
\mathbf{X}
is accepted by
any
level of the
\AIP
hierarchy applied to the template
\mathbf{K}_{n}
for
any

n\geq 3
—whence it follows that to fool any level of the
\AIP
hierarchy applied to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
one can simply take the clique
\mathbf{K}_{d+1}
(cf.
[
37
]
).

This clearly cannot be true for the stronger
\BA
hierarchy that, unlike
\AIP
, is sound in the limit. The obstruction is the condition (
15
). The goal is then to identify a class of more refined tensors
C
such that the associated homomorphism
\zeta_{C}
satisfies the above condition. To this end, we start by enforcing a stronger requirement on the projections on
C
:
The
k
-dimensional
(as opposed to
2
-dimensional)
projections of
C
should coincide
.
Note that we cannot require that
all
such projections be equal.
Indeed, already for
k=2
, if a matrix
M
is the projection of
C
onto some
2
-dimensional plane
xy
, then the projection of
C
onto the reflected plane
yx
is
M^{T}
. If these two projections need to be equal, it follows that
M
must be symmetric.
In addition,
M
is required to be affine and have zero diagonal, which clearly leads to a contradiction.
We then relax the hypothesis, by requiring that
only the
oriented

k
-dimensional projections be equal. We say that a tensor having this property is a
crystal
, as we next define.

Given
q,k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
[q]^{k}_{\rightarrow}
denote the set of increasing tuples in
[q]^{k}
; i.e.,
[q]^{k}_{\rightarrow}=\{(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in[q]^{k}\mbox{ s.t. }i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{k}\}
. We also set
[q]^{0}_{\to}=\{{\bm{\epsilon}}\}
. Observe that
[q]^{k}_{\to}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
k\leq q
.

Definition

(Formal version of Definition
3
)
.

Let
q,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,q\}
. A cubical tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
is a
k
-crystal
if
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{j}}\ast C

for each
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}
. In this case, the
k
-shadow
of
C
is the tensor
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C
(for some
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}
).

Remark 37
.

Given a not necessarily increasing tuple
\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{k}
, we can always find two tuples
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}
and
{\bm{\ell}}\in[k]^{k}
for which
\mathbf{j}=\mathbf{i}_{\bm{\ell}}
. Then, if
S
is the
k
-shadow of a
k
-crystal
C
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{j}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\bm{\ell}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\right)\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast S.

If
|{\bm{\ell}}|=k
(equivalently,
|\mathbf{j}|=k
), the tensor
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast S
is a reflection of
S
; i.e., it is obtained from
S
by simply permuting its modes (cf. Section
3.5
).
As a consequence, the definition above may be rephrased by asking that the projections of a
k
-crystal onto hyperplanes generated by
k
distinct modes should be equal
up to the reflection
associated with the orderings of the modes.

Let now
C
be a
k
-crystal, and let
S
be its
k
-shadow.
The condition (
15
) for the map
\zeta_{C}
associated with
C
becomes now a condition on the shadow
S
:
The only entries of
S
that are allowed to be nonzero are the ones whose coordinates are all distinct. We say that a tensor satisfying this requirement is
hollow
.

Figure 7
:
The tensor
S
from Example
39
.

Definition 38
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, and let
T\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbb{Z})
.
A tuple
\mathbf{a}\in[\mathbf{n}]
is a

tie
for
T
if
|\mathbf{a}|<k
and
\mathbf{a}\in\supp(T)
. We say that
T
is
hollow
if
T
does not have any ties.

In summary, we have (informally) shown that an affine
q
-dimensional
k
-crystal
C
of width
n
whose
k
-shadow is hollow yields a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\zeta_{C}
satisfying (
15
) and thus, through Theorem
2
, certifies that
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{n})=\textsc{Yes}
if
\mathbf{X}
has
q
vertices. (How to explicitly construct
\zeta_{C}
from a hollow-shadowed crystal
C
is discussed in more detail in the proof of Theorem
6
in Section
6
.) The problem is now to verify if such crystals actually exist. The next example shows that it is not possible to build a hollow-shadowed crystal whose width is too small.

Example 39
.

We now show by contradiction that, for any
q\geq 4
, it is not possible to build an affine
q
-dimensional
3
-crystal
C
of width
3
whose
3
-shadow
S
is hollow.

Suppose that such
C
exists.
First, observe that
S
belongs to
\mathcal{T}^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3}}(\mathbb{Z})
; i.e., it is a
3\times 3\times 3
integer tensor. Figure
7
shows
S
together with its three
2
-dimensional oriented projections; in grey are the cells that need to be zero to satisfy the hollowness requirement, while each of the other six cells is assigned a different colour.
11
11

11

The colours in Figure
7
are not related to the colours used in Section
2.2
and in Example
51
.

We shall see in Proposition
42
that, if
C
is a
3
-crystal, it also needs to be a
2
crystal; let
\tilde{S}
be the
2
-shadow of
C
. Then, for any
\mathbf{i}\in[3]^{2}_{\to}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pi^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\left(\Pi^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle 3\rangle}}\ast C\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny 3}{\ast}\Pi^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle 3\rangle}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle 3\rangle}_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\tilde{S}.

In other words,
S
is a
2
-crystal itself. It follows that the three oriented
2
-dimensional projections of
S
depicted in Figure
7
need to coincide:

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 27.56 -13.78 M 27.56 -13.78 L 27.56 0 L 41.34 0 L 41.34 -13.78 Z M 41.34 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
