{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -27.56 M 0 -27.56 L 0 -13.78 L 13.78 -13.78 L 13.78 -27.56 Z M 13.78 -13.78}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 -27.56 M 13.78 -27.56 L 13.78 -13.78 L 27.56 -13.78 L 27.56 -27.56 Z M 27.56 -13.78}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 27.56 -27.56 M 27.56 -27.56 L 27.56 -13.78 L 41.34 -13.78 L 41.34 -27.56 Z M 41.34 -13.78}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;.

This forces all six non-grey entries of
S
to be equal. On the other hand,
C
is affine, and we will see in Lemma
54
that
S
is affine, too. Since the entries of
S
are integers, this yields a contradiction.

As a consequence, taking an arbitrary digraph with high chromatic number is not enough for fooling the
\BA
hierarchy applied to AGC; in particular, unlike for the
\AIP
hierarchy,
one cannot simply use cliques as fooling instances.
This motivates the strategy, discussed in Section
2.3
(see also Section
6
), of using
shift digraphs
instead of cliques as fooling instances. To guarantee
\BA^{k}
acceptance for this more refined class of digraphs, it shall be enough to have hollow-shadowed crystals whose width is
sub-exponential
in
k
.
The result stated next is the
main technical contribution of this work, and it shows the existence of hollow-shadowed crystals whose width is
quadratic
in
k
.

Theorem

(Theorem
4
restated)
.

For any
k\leq q\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an affine
k
-crystal
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\frac{k^{2}+k}{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
with hollow
k
-shadow.

The core of this section is dedicated to the proof of the next result, from which Theorem
4
will follow via a simple
crystalisation
argument described in Section
5.3
.

Theorem

(Theorem
5
restated)
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a hollow affine
(k-1)
-crystal
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\frac{k^{2}+k}{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Our strategy to prove Theorem
5
shall be the following:

(
\spadesuit
1)

We start with a hollow affine
(k-1)
-dimensional
(k-2)
-crystal
U

of width
\frac{k^{2}-k}{2}
,
whose existence we assume by induction.

(
\spadesuit
2)

We build a (not necessarily hollow)
k
-dimensional
(k-1)
-crystal
V
whose shadow is
U
. This is done by using a general construction—described in Section
5.2
—that, given a “realistic system of shadows”
\mathcal{S}
, produces a “realisation” of
\mathcal{S}
, i.e., a
tensor whose projections are precisely the members of
\mathcal{S}
. In particular, the construction yields the crystalisation procedure of Section
5.3
.

(
\spadesuit
3)

We pad
V
with
k
layers of zeros in each dimension,
thus obtaining a wider tensor
W
that is still a
k
-dimensional
(k-1)
-crystal.

(
\spadesuit
4)

We modify
W
by adding to it certain transparent crystals, which we call
quartzes
, discussed in Section
5.4
. These crystals have the property of projecting an all-zero shadow, which implies in particular that the tensor
C
obtained after this process is still a crystal.

(
\spadesuit
5)

By carefully choosing the quartzes, we end up with
C
being hollow (as shown in Section
5.5
).

Remark 40
.

The step (
\spadesuit
3) has the consequence that the hollow crystals resulting from this process are progressively wider as
k
increases.
In fact, we are not able to build an affine hollow
(k-1)
-crystal
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
for
all
choices of
k
and
n
.
This is not a deficit of our methods: For instance, it follows from Example
39
that an affine hollow
2
-crystal in
\mathcal{T}^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3}}(\mathbb{Z})
cannot exist.

Remark 41
.

All of the steps (
\spadesuit
1)–(
\spadesuit
5) in the proof of Theorem
5
are
constructive
, in that they directly translate into an algorithm to find the required crystal. As a consequence, the proof of Theorem
4
on the existence of hollow-shadowed crystals of quadratic width is constructive, too.

5.1
Monotonicity of crystals

As a warm-up, we start by proving the next monotonicity property of crystals.

Proposition 42
.

Let
{q},n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{h},{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and suppose that
{h}\leq{k}<{q}
. Then any
{k}
-crystal in
\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
is also an
{h}
-crystal.

Before proving the proposition, we illustrate it with an example.

Example 43
.

Suppose for concreteness that
h=2
,
k=3
, and
q=6
, and let
C
be a
6
-dimensional
3
-crystal. To simplify the notation in this example, let
C_{ij\dots}
denote the projection of
C
onto the modes
(i,j,\dots)
.
To see why Proposition
42
is true, observe first that some of the oriented
2
-dimensional projections of
C
must be equal as an immediate consequence of the definition of a
3
-crystal.
For example, the fact that, say,
C_{12}=C_{23}
immediately follows from the fact that
C_{123}=C_{234}
—which, in turn, is implied by
C
being a
3
-crystal. However, in order to show that, say,
C_{12}=C_{56}
, one step is not sufficient:
Two
of the equalities enforced by
C
being a
3
-crystal need to be considered. For example, we may derive from
C_{123}=C_{456}
that
C_{12}=C_{45}
, and from
C_{345}=C_{456}
that
C_{45}=C_{56}
.

Thus, in some sense, Proposition
42
relies on the connectedness of the graph encoding the projections of the given
k
-crystal onto lower dimensional spaces.
The proof below formalises this idea in arbitrary dimensions via a simple minimality argument.
We point out that the assumption
k<q
is crucial for this argument to work—and for the result to hold. Indeed, it is easily verified from Definition
3
that
any
tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
is a
k
-crystal for
k=q
.

Proof of Proposition
42
.

We can assume that
{h}={k}-1
without loss of generality.
Given a tuple
\mathbf{i}\in[{q}]^{{h}}_{\to}
and
p\in[{q}]\setminus\set(\mathbf{i})
, we define
\mathbf{i}\boxplus p
as the tuple in
[{q}]^{k}_{\to}
obtained by inserting
p
into
\mathbf{i}
in the unique position that makes the resulting tuple monotonically increasing; in other words,
\mathbf{i}\boxplus p=(\mathbf{i}_{\langle\alpha\rangle},p,\mathbf{i}_{(\alpha+1,\dots,{h})})
, where
\alpha=|\{\beta\in[{h}]:i_{\beta}<p\}|
. Similarly, given
\mathbf{j}\in[{q}]^{k}_{\to}
and
r\in\set(\mathbf{j})
, we define
\mathbf{j}\boxminus r
as the tuple in
[{q}]^{{h}}_{\to}
obtained by removing
r
from
\mathbf{j}
.

Let
{C}
be a
{k}
-crystal in
\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
, and consider the tensor
S=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\langle{h}\rangle}\ast{C}
.
We now show that
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast{C}=S
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[{q}]^{{h}}_{\to}
, which implies the result. For the sake of contradiction,
let
\mathbf{i}\in[{q}]^{{h}}_{\to}
be a tuple such that
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast{C}\neq S
and such that the quantity
\mathbf{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{{h}}
is minimum among the set of tuples
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\in[{q}]^{{h}}_{\to}
for which
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\ast{C}\neq S
. Notice that the set
[{q}]\setminus\set(\mathbf{i})
has at least two elements as
{h}={k}-1\leq{q}-2
. Therefore, the numbers
\mu=\min([{q}]\setminus\set(\mathbf{i}))
and
\nu=\min([{q}]\setminus(\set(\mathbf{i})\cup\{\mu\}))
are well defined.
Consider the tuples
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{i}\boxplus\nu
and
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{a}\boxminus a_{{k}}\boxplus\mu
(where the operations are meant to be executed from the left to the right). By construction, we have
2\leq\nu\leq{k}+1
,
so
\nu-1\in[k]=\set({\langle k\rangle})
. Hence,
we can define the tuple
\mathbf{c}={\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus(\nu-1)
. By the definition of
\mu
and
\nu
, we have that
a_{\nu-1}=\nu
. This implies that
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{c}}=\mathbf{i}
, so

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;\neq\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{c}}}\ast{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{c}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{a}}\right)\ast{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{a}}\ast{C}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{c}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{b}}\right)\ast{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}}}\ast{C},

(16)

where the fourth equality uses that
{C}
is a
{k}
-crystal and that
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in[{q}]^{k}_{\to}
.
Observe that
a_{k}\geq\nu>\mu
, so
\mathbf{b}\leq\mathbf{a}
entrywise. It follows that
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}}\leq\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{c}}=\mathbf{i}
entrywise.

Assume first that
\nu\leq{k}
. In this case, we have
c_{{h}}={k}
. We claim that
i_{h}>\nu
; otherwise, we would have
i_{h}\leq\nu\leq{k}
, which would yield
\set(\mathbf{i})\cup\{\mu\}=[{k}]
since
\mathbf{i}
is monotonically increasing. This would force
\nu={k}+1
, a contradiction. In turn,
i_{h}>\nu
implies that
a_{k}=i_{h}
. In particular, this means that
a_{k}>\mu
, so
b_{k}<a_{k}
. We conclude that
b_{c_{{h}}}=b_{k}<a_{k}=i_{h}
.
Since, as noted above,
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}}\leq\mathbf{i}
entrywise, it follows that

\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{{h}}<\mathbf{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{{h}}
. Putting all together, we have derived that
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}}}\ast{C}\neq S
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{{h}}<\mathbf{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{{h}}
, thus contradicting our minimality assumption.

On the other hand, if
\nu={k}+1
, we deduce that
\mathbf{i}={\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus\mu
, so
\mathbf{a}={\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus\mu\boxplus({k}+1)
, thus yielding
a_{k}={k}+1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}{\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus\mu\boxplus({k}+1)\boxminus a_{k}\boxplus\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}{\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus\mu\boxplus({k}+1)\boxminus({k}+1)\boxplus\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}{\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus\mu\boxplus\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}{\langle{k}\rangle},

while
\mathbf{c}={\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus(\nu-1)={\langle{k}\rangle}\boxminus{k}={\langle{h}\rangle}
and, thus,
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}}={\langle{k}\rangle}_{{\langle{h}\rangle}}={\langle{h}\rangle}
. Then, (
5.1
) yields
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\langle{h}\rangle}\ast{C}\neq S
, which again contradicts our assumptions.

Hence, we have shown that
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast{C}=S
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[{q}]^{{h}}_{\to}
, and the proof is concluded.
∎

5.2
Systems of shadows

A crystal tensor has the property of projecting the same shadow onto each oriented hyperplane of appropriate dimension, cf. Definition
3
. The step (
\spadesuit
2) of the strategy to prove Theorem
5
requires reconstructing a crystal from its shadow. We now show how to accomplish this task. In fact, our approach shall be more general: In Theorem
45
, we characterise those sets of (lower-dimensional) tensors that can be realised as the oriented projections of a single (higher-dimensional) tensor. Then, we shall see in Section
5.3
(Corollary
50
) that this characterisation easily implies the existence of the crystal required in (
\spadesuit
2).

Definition 44
.

For
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
, a
(p,\mathbf{n})
-system of shadows
is a set
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}}
such that
S_{\mathbf{i}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}(\mathbb{Z})
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
.

•

\mathcal{S}
is a
realistic
system of shadows if

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}=\Pi_{\mathbf{s}}^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{j}}}\ast S_{\mathbf{j}}

for any

\displaystyle\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow},\;\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}\mbox{ such that }\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{s}}.

(17)

•

\mathcal{S}
is a
realisable
system of shadows if there exists a tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C=S_{\mathbf{i}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
.

In other words, a system of
p
-dimensional “shadow” tensors is realistic if the shadows are locally compatible with each other in the sense of the requirement (
17
), while it is realisable if it can actually be realised as the set of
p
-dimensional oriented projections of a single
q
-dimensional tensor. Notice that, for the set
\mathcal{S}
to be nonempty, we must have
p\leq q
. Observe also that the tensors
S_{\mathbf{i}}
and
C
are not required to be cubical.

Using Lemma
20
, it is not hard to check that a realisable system of shadows is always realistic. As stated in the next theorem, it turns out that the two conditions are in fact equivalent.

Theorem 45
.

Let
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
. A
(p,\mathbf{n})
-system of shadows is realistic if and only if it is realisable.

Theorem
45
is proved through a nested
induction—first on the dimension of the shadows
S_{\mathbf{i}}
(i.e.,
p
), and second on the
sum of the sizes of the modes of the tensor
C
that realises the shadows (i.e.,

\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}
). Lemmas
47
and
48
contain the base cases for the second and the first inductions, respectively. We note that the proof of Theorem
45
—as well as the proofs of Lemmas
47
and
48
—is constructive, as it directly provides a procedure to recover the tensor
C
realising a given realistic system of shadows
\mathcal{S}
. See also Example
51
, which illustrates this procedure applied to the problem of building a
4
-dimensional
2
-crystal having a given shadow.

In order to establish that a realistic system of shadows is always realisable—the non-trivial direction in Theorem
45
—we start by showing that the problem is invariant under
reflections
of the tensors involved.

Lemma 46
.

Let
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\bm{\ell}}\in[q]^{q}
be such that
|{\bm{\ell}}|=q
, and let

\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
. If every realistic
(p,\mathbf{n}_{\bm{\ell}})
-system of shadows is realisable then every realistic
(p,\mathbf{n})
-system of shadows is realisable.

Proof.

For purely typographical reasons, in this proof we will adopt an in-line notation for the operation of tuple projection: Given tuples
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c},\dots
of suitable lengths, we will denote the iterated projection
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{c}_{\dots}}}
by
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{b}\mathbf{c}\cdots
.

Since every permutation can be expressed as the composition of transpositions, it is
enough to consider the case that
{\bm{\ell}}
is a transposition; in particular,
{\bm{\ell}}{\bm{\ell}}={\langle q\rangle}
.
Let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}}
be a realistic
(p,\mathbf{n})
-system of shadows. For any
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
, let
\mathbf{i}^{+}
be the (unique) tuple in
[p]^{p}
such that
{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
. Let also
\mathbf{i}^{-}
be the (unique) tuple in
[p]^{p}
such that
\mathbf{i}^{+}{\mathbf{i}^{-}}=\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}={\langle p\rangle}
.
For each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
, define the tensor

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}=\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}.

(18)

Observe that
\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}\mathbf{i}}}(\mathbb{Z})
, so
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}=\{\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}}
is a
(p,\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}})
-system of shadows.
We claim that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is a realistic system.
To prove the claim, take
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
and
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
such that
\mathbf{i}\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{j}\mathbf{s}
. We need to show that

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}=\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{j}}.

(19)

Let
{\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{\beta}}\in[p-1]^{p-1}
be the (unique) tuples such that
\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{r}{\bm{\alpha}}}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
and
{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}={\bm{\beta}}{\bm{\alpha}}={\langle p-1\rangle}
. We claim that
\mathbf{j}^{-}{\mathbf{s}{\bm{\alpha}}}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
. Indeed, for any
x,y\in[p-1]
such that
x<y
we have

\displaystyle i^{-}{r{\alpha x}}<i^{-}{r{\alpha y}}\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\ell{i{i^{+}{i^{-}{r{\alpha x}}}}}<\ell{i{i^{+}{i^{-}{r{\alpha y}}}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\ell{i{{{r{\alpha x}}}}}<\ell{i{{{r{\alpha y}}}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\ell{j{{{s{\alpha x}}}}}<\ell{j{{{s{\alpha y}}}}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\ell{j{j^{+}{j^{-}{s{\alpha x}}}}}<\ell{j{j^{+}{j^{-}{s{\alpha y}}}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad j^{-}{s{\alpha x}}<j^{-}{s{\alpha y}},

thus proving the claim.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1045_0607}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\left(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{r}{{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{\beta}}}}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{r}{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}_{\bm{\beta}}\overset{\tiny p-1}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{r}{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{r}{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}_{\bm{\beta}}\ast\left(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{r}{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}\right)

(20)

and, similarly,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{j}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{\mathbf{s}{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}_{\bm{\beta}}\ast\left(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{\mathbf{j}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{j}^{-}{\mathbf{s}{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{\mathbf{j}^{+}}}}\right).

(21)

Let us now focus on the tuples
{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}},{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{\mathbf{j}^{+}}}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
and
\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{r}{\bm{\alpha}}},\mathbf{j}^{-}{\mathbf{s}{\bm{\alpha}}}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
. Observe that

\displaystyle{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}{\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{r}{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{{{\mathbf{r}{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{{{\mathbf{s}{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{\mathbf{j}^{+}{\mathbf{j}^{-}{\mathbf{s}{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}.

Using that
\mathcal{S}
is a realistic system, we deduce

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}{\mathbf{r}{\bm{\alpha}}}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{\mathbf{j}+}}}}_{\mathbf{j}^{-}{\mathbf{s}{\bm{\alpha}}}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{j}{\mathbf{j}+}}}.

(22)

Combining (
5.2
), (
21
), and (
22
), and recalling that
\mathbf{i}\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{j}\mathbf{s}
, yields (
19
), thus proving that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is a realistic
(p,\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}})
-system of shadows, as claimed. From the hypothesis of the lemma, we deduce that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is realisable, so there exists a tensor
\tilde{C}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\tilde{C}=\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
. Define
C=\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast\tilde{C}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbb{Z})
(where we are using that
{\bm{\ell}}{\bm{\ell}}={\langle q\rangle}
). Given
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
, we find

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast\tilde{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}{\mathbf{i}^{-}}}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast\tilde{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}{\mathbf{i}^{-}}}}\overset{\tiny q}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast\tilde{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{+}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny q}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{{\bm{\ell}}}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\bm{\ell}}}_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}\ast\tilde{C})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\ast\tilde{S}_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}.

(23)

Notice that
{\bm{\ell}}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}{\mathbf{i}^{-}}}}}=\mathbf{i}
, which is an increasing tuple. Hence,
\left({\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}\right)^{+}=\mathbf{i}^{-}
and, consequently,
\left({\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}\right)^{-}=\mathbf{i}^{+}
. It follows from (
18
) that

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{{\bm{\ell}}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}{\mathbf{i}^{-}}}}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{+}}\ast S_{{\bm{\ell}}{{\bm{\ell}}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}{\mathbf{i}^{-}}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{+}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}.

(24)

Combining (
5.2
) and (
24
) yields

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{+}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}{\mathbf{i}^{+}}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{-}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{i}^{+}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}\mathbf{i}}_{{\langle p\rangle}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_identity_Pi}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}S_{\mathbf{i}},

which concludes the proof that
\mathcal{S}
is a realisable system of shadows.
∎

The next result establishes the base case for the second
induction in the proof of Theorem
45
. Its proof is a simple connectivity argument for the shadows’ modes, analogous to the one used to prove Proposition
42
.

Lemma 47
.

A realistic
(p,\mathbf{1}_{q})
-system of shadows is realisable for any
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}}
be a realistic
(p,\mathbf{1}_{q})
-system of shadows. For any
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
,
S_{\mathbf{i}}\in\mathcal{T}^{(\mathbf{1}_{q})_{\mathbf{i}}}(\mathbb{Z})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}_{p}}(\mathbb{Z})
. We claim that
S_{\mathbf{i}}=S_{\mathbf{j}}
for any
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
. Define, for each pair
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
, their
distance

\operatorname{d}(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})
as the cardinality of the set
\{t\in[p]:i_{t}\neq j_{t}\}
. Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that the claim is false, and let
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
attain the minimum distance among all pairs
\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}
for which
S_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\neq S_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}
. Let
\alpha=\max\{t\in[p]:i_{t}\neq j_{t}\}
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
i_{\alpha}<j_{\alpha}
, and define a new tuple
{\bm{\ell}}\in[q]^{p}
obtained from
\mathbf{i}
by replacing
i_{\alpha}
with
j_{\alpha}
. Observe that
i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{\alpha-1}<i_{\alpha}<j_{\alpha}<j_{\alpha+1}=i_{\alpha+1}<i_{\alpha+2}<\dots<i_{p}
, so
{\bm{\ell}}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
. Letting
\mathbf{r}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
be obtained from
{\langle p\rangle}
by deleting its
\alpha
-th entry, observe that
\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}={\bm{\ell}}_{\mathbf{r}}
. Using that
\mathcal{S}
is a realistic system, we obtain
\Pi^{\mathbf{1}_{p}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}=\Pi_{\mathbf{r}}^{\mathbf{1}_{p}}\ast S_{\bm{\ell}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{1}_{p-1}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{1}_{p}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{1}_{p-1}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{1}_{p}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\bm{\ell}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}\ast S_{\bm{\ell}},

so
S_{\bm{\ell}}=S_{\mathbf{i}}\neq S_{\mathbf{j}}
. But this contradicts the choice of the pair
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})
, as
\operatorname{d}({\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{j})=\operatorname{d}(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j})-1
. Hence, the claim is true.
We can then define a tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
by setting
E_{\mathbf{1}_{q}}\ast C=E_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}
for any
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
. In this way, we get

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{1}_{q}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{1}_{p}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}.

We conclude that
\Pi^{\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C=S_{\mathbf{i}}
for any
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
, which means that
\mathcal{S}
is a realisable system.
∎

The next result establishes the base case for the first
induction in the proof of Theorem
45
.

Lemma 48
.

A realistic
(1,\mathbf{n})
-system of shadows is realisable for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
.

Example 49
.

For
q=2
, the statement above expresses the fact that, given two integer vectors
S_{1}
of length
n_{1}
and
S_{2}
of length
n_{2}
such that the sums of the entries of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
coincide, there exists an
n_{1}\times n_{2}
integer matrix
C
whose row-sum and column-sum vectors are
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, respectively.

Proof of Lemma
48
.

If
q=1
, the result is trivially true; indeed, in this case, the vector
C=S_{1}
witnesses that the given system of shadows
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1}\}
is realisable. Hence, assume
q\geq 2
.
Notice that
[q]^{1}_{\rightarrow}=[q]
, so each element of
[q]^{1}_{\rightarrow}
is a single number.
We prove the statement by induction on
\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}
.
If
\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}=q
, then
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{1}_{q}
, and the result follows from Lemma
47
. Suppose that
\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}\geq q+1
.
Using Lemma
46
, we can assume
n_{q}\geq 2
without loss of generality. Let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{i}\}_{i\in[q]}
be a realistic
(1,\mathbf{n})
-system of shadows; observe that
S_{i}
is a vector in
\mathcal{T}^{n_{i}}(\mathbb{Z})
for each
i\in[q]
.
Set
\ell=E_{n_{q}}\ast S_{q}
(i.e.,
\ell
is the last entry of
S_{q}
), and consider a new family of tensors
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}=\{\tilde{S}_{i}\}_{i\in[q]}
defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}S_{i}-\ell E_{n_{i}}&\mbox{if }i\in[q-1]\\
(E_{1}\ast S_{q},\dots,E_{n_{q}-1}\ast S_{q})&\mbox{if }i=q.\end{array}\right.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbf{n}-E_{q}
and notice that
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
since
n_{q}\geq 2
. We have that
S_{i}\in\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{n}_{i}}(\mathbb{Z})
for each
i\in[q]
, so
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is a
(1,\tilde{\mathbf{n}})
-system of shadows.

We now show that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is realistic.
By definition,
[1]^{0}_{\rightarrow}=\{{\bm{\epsilon}}\}
, so we only need to show that

\displaystyle\Pi^{\tilde{n}_{i}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\tilde{S}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\tilde{n}_{j}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\tilde{S}_{j}

\displaystyle\forall\,i,j\in[q].

(25)

We claim that

\displaystyle\Pi^{\tilde{n}_{i}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\tilde{S}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n_{i}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast S_{i}-\ell

\displaystyle\forall\,i\in[q].

(26)

Then, (
25
) will follow from the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is a realistic system. If
i\in[q-1]
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\tilde{n}_{i}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\tilde{S}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n_{i}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast(S_{i}-\ell E_{n_{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{Pi_epsilon_all_one}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n_{i}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast S_{i}-\ell,

so (
26
) holds in this case. Moreover,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\tilde{n}_{q}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\tilde{S}_{q}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n_{q}-1}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast(E_{1}\ast S_{q},\dots,E_{n_{q}-1}\ast S_{q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{Pi_epsilon_all_one}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{b\in[n_{q}-1]}E_{b}\ast S_{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\mathbf{1}_{n_{q}}\ast S_{q}-\ell

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{Pi_epsilon_all_one}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n_{q}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast S_{q}-\ell,

so (
26
) holds in this case as well. We conclude that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is indeed a realistic system.

Since
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}=\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}-1
, we have from the inductive hypothesis that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is realisable, so there exists a tensor
\tilde{C}\in\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}_{i}\ast\tilde{C}=\tilde{S}_{i}
for each
i\in[q]
. Define a tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbb{Z})
by setting, for each
\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]
,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\ell&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{n}\\
0&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{b}\neq\mathbf{n}\;\mbox{ and }\;b_{q}=n_{q}\\
E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{C}&\mbox{ if }b_{q}\neq n_{q}.\end{array}\right.

(Notice that the last line of the right-hand side of the above expression is well defined as, if
b_{q}\neq n_{q}
, then
\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}]
.)
Take
i\in[q]
; we claim that
\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\ast C=S_{i}
. For
a\in[n_{i}]
, we find

\displaystyle E_{a}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
b_{i}=a\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C.

For
i\neq q
, this yields

\displaystyle E_{a}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
b_{i}=a\\
b_{q}=n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
b_{i}=a\\
b_{q}\neq n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1230_0507}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\ell\cdot\delta_{a,n_{i}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}]\\
b_{i}=a\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{C}

(where
\delta_{a,n_{i}}
is
1
if
a=n_{i}
,
0
otherwise)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\ell\cdot\delta_{a,n_{i}}+E_{a}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}_{i}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\ell\cdot\delta_{a,n_{i}}+E_{a}\ast\tilde{S}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\ell\cdot\delta_{a,n_{i}}+E_{a}\ast(S_{i}-\ell E_{n_{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{a}\ast S_{i}.

For
i=q
, if
a=n_{q}
we get

\displaystyle E_{a}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{q}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
b_{q}=n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1230_0507}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\ell\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{a}\ast S_{q},

while if
a\neq n_{q}
we get

\displaystyle E_{a}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{q}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
b_{q}=a\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1230_0507}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}]\\
b_{q}=a\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{a}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}_{q}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{a}\ast\tilde{S}_{q}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{a}\ast(E_{1}\ast S_{q},\dots,E_{n_{q}-1}\ast S_{q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{a}\ast S_{q}.

It follows that
\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{i}\ast C=S_{i}
, as claimed. Therefore,
\mathcal{S}
is a realisable system.
∎

Proof of Theorem
45
.

Let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}}
be a realisable system of shadows; i.e., there exists
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C=S_{\mathbf{i}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
. For any
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
and
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
such that
\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{s}}
, we find

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{s}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{j}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast S_{\mathbf{j}},

which shows that
\mathcal{S}
is a realistic system. Hence, the “if” part of the statement is true. Next, we focus on the “only if” part.

We prove the result by nested induction, first on
p
and second on
\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}
. For
p=1
, the result follows from Lemma
48
. Suppose that
p\geq 2
.
For
\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}=q
(which implies
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{1}_{q}
), the result follows from Lemma
47
. Suppose that
\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}\geq q+1
. Using Lemma
46
, we can safely assume
n_{q}\geq 2
.
If
q=1
, then
[q]^{p}_{\to}=\emptyset
and the statement is trivially true, so we can assume
q\geq 2
.
Let
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\to}}
be a realistic
(p,\mathbf{n})
-system of shadows; we need to show that
\mathcal{S}
is realisable.

Set

\hat{\mathbf{n}}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{q-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{q-1}
. For any
\mathbf{i}\in[q-1]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
, we define
\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}(\mathbb{Z})
by
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}}=E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast S_{(\mathbf{i},q)}
for each
\mathbf{a}\in[\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]
. Observe that the last expression is well defined, as
\mathbf{i}\in[q-1]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
implies that
(\mathbf{i},q)\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
.
We claim that the family
\hat{\mathcal{S}}=\{\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q-1]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}}
is a realistic
(p-1,\hat{\mathbf{n}})
-system of shadows. Take
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q-1]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
and
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in[p-1]^{p-2}_{\rightarrow}
such that
\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{s}}
. For any
\mathbf{a}\in[\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}]
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{(\mathbf{b},n_{q})}\ast S_{(\mathbf{i},q)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in[{\mathbf{n}}_{(\mathbf{i},q)}]\\
\mathbf{c}_{(\mathbf{r},p)}=(\mathbf{a},n_{q})\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast S_{(\mathbf{i},q)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{(\mathbf{i},q)}}_{(\mathbf{r},p)}\ast S_{(\mathbf{i},q)}

(30)

and, similarly,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{j}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{(\mathbf{j},q)}}_{(\mathbf{s},p)}\ast S_{(\mathbf{j},q)}.

(31)

We now use the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is a realistic system. In particular, we apply the requirement (
17
) to the tuples
(\mathbf{i},q),(\mathbf{j},q)\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
and
(\mathbf{r},p),(\mathbf{s},p)\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
(note that
(\mathbf{i},q)_{(\mathbf{r},p)}=(\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}},q)=(\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{s}},q)=(\mathbf{j},q)_{(\mathbf{s},p)}
). Since
(\mathbf{a},n_{q})\in[\mathbf{n}_{(\mathbf{i},q)_{(\mathbf{r},p)}}]
, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{(\mathbf{i},q)}}_{(\mathbf{r},p)}\ast S_{(\mathbf{i},q)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{(\mathbf{j},q)}}_{(\mathbf{s},p)}\ast S_{(\mathbf{j},q)}.

Combining this with (
5.2
) and (
31
) yields

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{j}}.

We conclude that
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
is a realistic system, as claimed.
It follows from the inductive hypothesis that
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
is realisable, so we can find a tensor
\hat{C}\in\mathcal{T}^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\hat{C}=\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q-1]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
.
Let now
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbf{n}-E_{q}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
. For any
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
, define a tensor
\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}(\mathbb{Z})
as follows:
If
i_{p}\neq q
(in which case
\mathbf{i}\in[q-1]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
) we set
\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}=S_{\mathbf{i}}-\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\hat{C}
; if
i_{p}=q
, for
\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]
, we set
E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}=E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}
(where the last expression is well defined as
[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}]\subseteq[\mathbf{n}]
, so
[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\subseteq[\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}]
).
We claim that the family
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}=\{\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}}
is a realistic
(p,\tilde{\mathbf{n}})
-system of shadows. To show that the claim is true, we shall first prove that the equation

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}&\mbox{ if }i_{r_{p-1}}=q\\
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}})&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.

is satisfied for any
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
, any
\mathbf{r}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
, and any
\mathbf{a}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}]
. First, notice that, if
i_{p}=q
,

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}[\tilde{n}_{i_{1}}]\times\dots\times[\tilde{n}_{i_{p-1}}]\times[\tilde{n}_{i_{p}}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}[{n}_{i_{1}}]\times\dots\times[n_{i_{p-1}}]\times[{n}_{q}-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\{\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}]:b_{p}\neq n_{q}\}

while, if
i_{p}\neq q
,
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}=\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}
, so
[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]=[\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]=[\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}]
.
Suppose that
i_{r_{p-1}}=q
. In this case, we have
r_{p-1}=p
and
i_{p}=q
. Hence,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{a}\\
b_{p}\neq n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}},

so (
5.2
) holds in this case. Suppose now that
i_{r_{p-1}}\neq q
. This can happen either if
i_{p}\neq q
(
case
a
), or if
i_{p}=q
and
r_{p-1}\neq p
(
case
b
), and it implies that
\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}\in[q-1]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
. In
case
a
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast(S_{\mathbf{i}}-\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\hat{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\overset{\tiny p}{\ast}\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\hat{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}\ast\hat{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}},

where the last equality follows from the property of
\hat{C}
. So, (
5.2
) holds in this case. In
case
b
, we must have
\mathbf{r}={\langle p-1\rangle}
. Hence,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}=\mathbf{a}\\
b_{p}\neq n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-E_{(\mathbf{a},n_{q})}\ast S_{(\mathbf{i}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}},q)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}),

where the penultimate equality comes from the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
and from the fact that, in this case,
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}=\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}
, so
\mathbf{a}\in[\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}]
. We conclude that (
5.2
) also holds in
case
b
. Using (
5.2
) and the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is a realistic system, we easily conclude that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is a realistic system, too. Indeed, take
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
and
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in[p]^{p-1}_{\rightarrow}
such that
\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}=\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{s}}
, and choose
\mathbf{a}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}}]
. Observe that
i_{r_{p-1}}=j_{s_{p-1}}
. If
i_{r_{p-1}}=q
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast S_{\mathbf{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{j}};

otherwise,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}}_{\mathbf{r}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{r}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast(\Pi^{\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast S_{\mathbf{j}}-\hat{S}_{\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{s}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{j}}}_{\mathbf{s}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{j}}.

It follows that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is indeed a realistic system, as claimed.
Since
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}=\mathbf{n}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{q}-1
, we can then apply the inductive hypothesis to deduce that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is realisable, so there exists a tensor
\tilde{C}\in\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\tilde{C}=\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
.

We now define a tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbb{Z})
by setting, for each
\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]
,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}E_{\mathbf{b}_{\langle q-1\rangle}}\ast\hat{C}&\mbox{ if }&b_{q}=n_{q}\\[5.0pt]
E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{C}&\mbox{ if }&b_{q}\neq n_{q}.\end{array}\right.

Take
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
and
\mathbf{a}\in[\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{i}}]
. To conclude the proof, we need to show that

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}.

(36)

Observe that

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
b_{q}=n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
b_{q}\neq n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_2124_0507}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
b_{q}=n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}_{{\langle q-1\rangle}}}\ast\hat{C}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{C}.

(37)

Let us denote the first and the second summand of the rightmost expression in (
37
) by
\alpha
and
\beta
, respectively.
Suppose first that
i_{p}=q
. If
a_{p}\neq n_{q}
, we see that
\alpha=0
, so

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1311_07062022}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}};

if
a_{p}=n_{q}
, we get
\beta=0
, so

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1311_07062022}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
b_{q}=n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}_{{\langle q-1\rangle}}}\ast\hat{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}_{{\langle q-1\rangle}}}\ast\hat{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in[\hat{\mathbf{n}}]\\
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{i}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}}=\mathbf{a}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\hat{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}}\ast\hat{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}}\ast\hat{S}_{\mathbf{i}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{(\mathbf{a}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}},n_{q})}\ast S_{(\mathbf{i}_{{\langle p-1\rangle}},q)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}.

Suppose now that
i_{p}\neq q
, in which case
\mathbf{i}\in[q-1]^{p}_{\rightarrow}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\mathbf{n}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
b_{q}=n_{q}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}_{{\langle q-1\rangle}}}\ast\hat{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in[\hat{\mathbf{n}}]\\
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\hat{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\hat{C},

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\tilde{\mathbf{n}}]\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\tilde{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\tilde{S}_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast(S_{\mathbf{i}}-\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\hat{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}-E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{\mathbf{n}}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\hat{C},

and it follows that

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1311_07062022}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\alpha+\beta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast S_{\mathbf{i}}.

Therefore, (
36
) holds,
\mathcal{S}
is realisable, and the proof is concluded.∎

5.3
Crystalisation

One easily derives from Theorem
45
a
crystalisation
procedure, which turns a given crystal
S
into a new crystal whose shadow is
S
. This is precisely what is needed to complete the step (
\spadesuit
2) of the proof of Theorem
5
.

Corollary 50
.

Let
n,q\in\mathbb{N}
, let
k\in[q]
, and let
S\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
be a
(k-1)
-crystal. Then there exists a
k
-crystal
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
whose
k
-shadow is
S
.

Proof.

Consider the
(k,n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q})
-system of shadows
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{\mathbf{i}}\}_{\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}}
obtained by setting
S_{\mathbf{i}}=S
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}
. The fact that
S
is a
(k-1)
-crystal immediately implies that
\mathcal{S}
is a realistic system of shadows. Using Theorem
45
, we deduce that
\mathcal{S}
is realisable—i.e., there exists a tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C=S
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}
. It follows that
C
is a
k
-crystal, whose
k
-shadow is
S
.
∎

Before proceeding to the next steps towards the proof of Theorem
5
, we illustrate the crystalisation procedure on a concrete example, by showing how to produce a
4
-dimensional
2
-crystal having a given shadow through the construction described in Section
5.2
.

Example 51
.

Throughout this example, we shall indicate the numbers
-2
,
-1
,
{0}
,
1
,
2
, and
3
by the colours blue, green, light grey, yellow, orange, and red, respectively.

Take
n=3
,
q=4
, and
k=2
in the statement of Corollary
50
. The goal is to build a
2
-crystal
C\in\mathcal{T}^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{4}}(\mathbb{Z})
whose
2
-shadow is the matrix
(which is easily shown to be a
1
-crystal, as the row- and column-sum vectors coincide).
To this end, we consider the
(2,3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{4})
-system of shadows
\mathcal{S}
whose members are all equal to

.
\mathcal{S}
is trivially realistic. The goal is to show that it is realisable; indeed, the tensor
C\in\mathcal{T}^{3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{4}}(\mathbb{Z})
witnessing this fact would be the crystal we seek.
Following the proof of Theorem
45
, we create two auxiliary systems of shadows
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
.
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
is a
(1,3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3})
-system—i.e., both the shadows and the tensor that is claimed to realise
them have one fewer dimension than those for the original system
\mathcal{S}
. In particular, we see from the proof that all members of
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
are the same vector
.
Again, it is not hard to verify that
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
is a realistic system. To check that it is realisable, we only need to find a
3
-dimensional tensor of width
3
such that summing its entries along all three modes yields
.
Either by inspection or using the proof of Lemma
48
, we find that

\displaystyle\hat{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\mbox{
