\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

This system is also realistic, and it is such that the sum of the sizes of the modes of the tensor
\tilde{C}
that is claimed to realise it is strictly smaller than the corresponding quantity for the system
\mathcal{S}
. At this point, we simply iterate the process, by repeatedly “slicing”
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
into a system of
1
-dimensional shadows
(which we handle through Lemma
48
) and a smaller system of
2
-dimensional shadows,
until we end up with a system such that the sizes of all modes are shrunk to
1
, so that the tensor realising it is a single number (see Lemma
47
).
Throughout this process, Lemma
46
guarantees that the tensors can be rotated in a way that we slice along the rightmost mode, thus avoiding complications with the orientations of the shadows.
In this way, we find that the system
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is realised by the tensor
\tilde{C}
whose two blocks are

and the all-zero
3\times 3\times 3
tensor, respectively. Finally, to obtain a tensor
C
realising the initial system
\mathcal{S}
(i.e., a
4
-dimensional
2
-crystal having shadow
), we glue together
\tilde{C}
and
\hat{C}
.
The result is shown in Figure
8
.

Figure 8
:
A
4
-dimensional
2
-crystal having shadow

.

5.4
Quartzes

The crystalisation procedure destroys hollowness: Even when the crystal
S
in the statement of Corollary
50
is hollow, the new crystal
C
resulting from the crystalisation is not hollow in general—as it is clear from Example
51
.
There does not appear to be
a natural way of modifying the inductive construction in Section
5.2

to require that hollowness be preserved along the process. Hence, to achieve hollowness, we employ a second, separate procedure—step (
\spadesuit
4)—which consists in applying multiple
local modifications
to the crystal resulting from step (
\spadesuit
2) (after expanding it with layers of zeros in step (
\spadesuit
3)). These modifications are associated with certain transparent crystals defined next.

Definition 52
.

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}
be such that
a_{i}\neq b_{i}
for each
i\in[k]
. Given
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, let
h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
be the tuple in
[n]^{k}
whose
i
-th entry is
a_{i}
if
z_{i}=0
,
b_{i}
otherwise. The
quartz

Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}
is the tensor in
\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
defined by
Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}
. Equivalently,
E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}
for each
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, and all other entries are zero.

Let the symbol “
\odot
” indicate the entrywise multiplication of tuples having the same length.

Remark 53
.

It is straightforward to check that, for any two tuples
\mathbf{z},\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
,
\mathbf{z}=\hat{\mathbf{z}}
if and only if
h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=h(\hat{\mathbf{z}};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
.
We can write

\displaystyle h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=(\mathbf{1}_{k}-\mathbf{z})\odot\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{z}\odot\mathbf{b}.

(39)

Notice that the operation of tuple projection distributes over “
\odot
”, in the sense that
(\mathbf{u}\odot\mathbf{v})_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{i}}\odot\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Hence, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\mathbf{j}\in[k]^{\ell}
,

\displaystyle[h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})]_{\mathbf{j}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1835_4_oct}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}[(\mathbf{1}_{k}-\mathbf{z})\odot\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{z}\odot\mathbf{b}]_{\mathbf{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(\mathbf{1}_{\ell}-\mathbf{z}_{\mathbf{j}})\odot\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{j}}+\mathbf{z}_{\mathbf{j}}\odot\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1835_4_oct}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}h(\mathbf{z}_{\mathbf{j}};\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{j}},\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{j}}).

(40)

We will need the following simple lemma on crystals.

Lemma 54
.

Let
q,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,q\}
,
let
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
be a
k
-crystal, and let
S
be its
k
-shadow. Then
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast C=\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast S
. In particular,
C
is affine if and only if
S
is affine.

Proof.

Observe that
{\langle k\rangle}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}
and
{\langle k\rangle}_{\bm{\epsilon}}={\bm{\epsilon}}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle k\rangle}_{\bm{\epsilon}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\langle k\rangle}\right)\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\langle k\rangle}\ast C\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast S,

as required. Then, the last part of the statement directly follows from the definition of an affine tensor (Definition
36
).
∎

The next proposition collects certain properties of quartzes that shall be useful later.

Proposition 55
.

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}
be such that
a_{i}\neq b_{i}
for each
i\in[k]
. Then

(i)

\supp(Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}})=\{a_{1},b_{1}\}\times\{a_{2},b_{2}\}\times\dots\times\{a_{k},b_{k}\}
.

(ii)

E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=1
.

(iii)

\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\ell}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=Q_{\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}}}
for any
{\bm{\ell}}\in[k]^{k}
such that
|{\bm{\ell}}|=k
.

(iv)

Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}
is a
(k-1)
-crystal, and its
(k-1)
-shadow is the all-zero tensor in
\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k-1}}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(v)

\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=0
.

Proof.

To prove (
i
), take
S=\{a_{1},b_{1}\}\times\dots\times\{a_{k},b_{k}\}\subseteq[n]^{k}
. The map
\mathbf{z}\mapsto h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
yields a bijection between
\{0,1\}^{k}
and
S
. Hence,

\displaystyle\supp(Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\bigcup_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\supp(E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\bigcup_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\{{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\bigcup_{\mathbf{s}\in S}\{\mathbf{s}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}S.

To prove (
ii
), observe that
\mathbf{a}=h(\mathbf{0}_{k};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
, whence we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(-1)^{\mathbf{0}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}1.

To prove (
iii
),
observe that

\displaystyle Q_{\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}_{\bm{\ell}}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}E_{h(\mathbf{z}_{\bm{\ell}};\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}E_{h(\mathbf{z}_{\bm{\ell}};\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}})},

(41)

where the second equality is obtained by noting that summing over
\mathbf{z}
is equivalent to summing over
\mathbf{z}_{\bm{\ell}}
, since
|{\bm{\ell}}|=k
. On the other hand, letting
\mathbf{j}\in[k]^{k}
be the tuple for which
{\bm{\ell}}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{j}_{\bm{\ell}}={\langle k\rangle}
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\ell}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in[n]^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\ell}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}})E_{\mathbf{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in[n]^{k}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{d}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{d}_{\bm{\ell}}=\mathbf{c}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{d}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}\bigg)E_{\mathbf{c}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in[n]^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}})E_{\mathbf{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in[n]^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}})E_{\mathbf{c}_{\bm{\ell}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in[n]^{k}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}(E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})})E_{\mathbf{c}_{\bm{\ell}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in[n]^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})})E_{\mathbf{c}_{\bm{\ell}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}E_{[h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})]_{\bm{\ell}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1817_60oct}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}E_{h(\mathbf{z}_{\bm{\ell}};\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}})}.

(42)

Combining (
5.4
) and (
5.4
), we obtain
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\ell}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}=Q_{\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}}}
.

To prove (
iv
), observe that, for any
\mathbf{c}\in[n]^{k-1}
,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{d}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{d}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}=\mathbf{c}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{d}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{d\in[n]}E_{(\mathbf{c},d)}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}}(-1)^{\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\sum_{d\in[n]}E_{(\mathbf{c},d)}\ast E_{h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}.

(43)

In order for a tuple
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
to give a nonzero contribution to the sum in the right-hand side of (
5.4
), we must have that
(\mathbf{c},d)=h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})
for some
d\in[n]
, which implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(\mathbf{c},d)_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}[h(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})]_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1817_60oct}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}h(\mathbf{z}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}};\mathbf{a}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}},\mathbf{b}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}).

In particular,
\mathbf{z}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}=\tilde{\mathbf{z}}
for some
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}
such that
\mathbf{c}=h(\tilde{\mathbf{z}};\mathbf{a}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}},\mathbf{b}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}})
. Then, it follows from Remark
53
that such tuple
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}
is unique. Notice that
h((\tilde{\mathbf{z}},0);\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=(\mathbf{c},a_{k})
and
h((\tilde{\mathbf{z}},1);\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})=(\mathbf{c},b_{k})
.
As a consequence, we can simplify (
5.4
) to yield

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{(\tilde{\mathbf{z}},z)^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\sum_{d\in[n]}E_{(\mathbf{c},d)}\ast E_{h((\tilde{\mathbf{z}},z);\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(-1)^{(\tilde{\mathbf{z}},0)^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\sum_{d\in[n]}E_{(\mathbf{c},d)}\ast E_{h((\tilde{\mathbf{z}},0);\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}+(-1)^{(\tilde{\mathbf{z}},1)^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\sum_{d\in[n]}E_{(\mathbf{c},d)}\ast E_{h((\tilde{\mathbf{z}},1);\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(-1)^{(\tilde{\mathbf{z}},0)^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\sum_{d\in[n]}E_{(\mathbf{c},d)}\ast E_{(\mathbf{c},a_{k})}+(-1)^{(\tilde{\mathbf{z}},1)^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\sum_{d\in[n]}E_{(\mathbf{c},d)}\ast E_{(\mathbf{c},b_{k})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(-1)^{(\tilde{\mathbf{z}},0)^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}+(-1)^{(\tilde{\mathbf{z}},1)^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(-1)^{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k-1}}-(-1)^{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k-1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}0.

It follows that
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}
is the all-zero tensor. Take now
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k-1}_{\to}
, and let
p
be the unique element of
[k]\setminus\set(\mathbf{i})
. Consider the tuple
{\bm{\ell}}=(\mathbf{i},p)\in[k]^{k}
, and notice that
|{\bm{\ell}}|=k
and
\mathbf{i}={\bm{\ell}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\ell}}\right)\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\ell}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{P}.\ref{prop_basic_stuff_quartzes}\eqref{lem_part_inveriance_under_tuple_projection}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle k-1\rangle}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{a}_{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\ell}}},

which is the all-zero tensor as proved above. This shows that
Q_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}}
is a
(k-1)
-crystal having the all-zero tensor as its
(k-1)
-shadow.

Finally, (
v
) directly follows from (
iv
) by applying Lemma
54
.
∎

5.5
Crystals with hollow shadows

We now have all the ingredients for implementing the steps (
\spadesuit
1)–(
\spadesuit
5), thus completing the proof of Theorem
5
. Once that is established, the existence of hollow-shadowed crystals of quadratic width (Theorem
4
) can be easily derived.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We use induction over
k
. For
k=1
, the tensor
C=1
works. For the inductive step, suppose that
k\geq 2
.
Let
\hat{n}=\frac{k^{2}-k}{2}
and
n=\hat{n}+k=\frac{k^{2}+k}{2}
. By the inductive hypothesis, we find a hollow affine
(k-2)
-crystal
U\in\mathcal{T}^{\hat{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k-1}}(\mathbb{Z})
(
\spadesuit
1). Using Corollary
50
, we deduce that there exists a (not necessarily hollow)
(k-1)
-crystal
V\in\mathcal{T}^{\hat{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
whose
(k-1)
-shadow is
U
(
\spadesuit
2). By Lemma
54
,
V
is affine, too.
Consider now the tensor
W\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
defined by setting, for each
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
,
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast W=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast V
if
\set(\mathbf{a})\subseteq[\hat{n}]
,
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast W=0
otherwise; i.e.,
W
is obtained by padding
V
with
k
layers of zeros on each mode (
\spadesuit
3). Similarly, define
Z\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k-1}}(\mathbb{Z})
by setting, for each
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k-1}
,
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Z=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast U
if
\set(\mathbf{a})\subseteq[\hat{n}]
,
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Z=0
otherwise. Observe that
\supp(U)=\supp(Z)
, so
U
being hollow implies
Z
being hollow as well. We claim that
W
is a
(k-1)
-crystal whose
(k-1)
-shadow is
Z
. Indeed, for any
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k-1}_{\to}
and
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k-1}
,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast W

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast W=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[\hat{n}]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{\hat{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast V&\mbox{ if }\set(\mathbf{a})\subseteq[\hat{n}]\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast U&\mbox{ if }\set(\mathbf{a})\subseteq[\hat{n}]\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Z,

so
\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast W=Z
, as wanted. Clearly, the padding operation does not change the sum of the entries in the tensor, so
W
is affine. Consider the tuple
\mathbf{y}=(\hat{n}+1,\hat{n}+2,\dots,n)\in[n]^{k}
, and define (
\spadesuit
4) the tensor

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=W-\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in[\hat{n}]^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{d}}\ast W)Q_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{y}}.

(44)

Note that
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
.
We shall prove that
C
is a hollow affine
(k-1)
-crystal. Recall that
W
is an affine
(k-1)
-crystal.
Since tensor projection is a linear operation, crystals are preserved under linear combinations. Hence,
it follows from Proposition
55
(
iv
) that
C
is a
(k-1)
-crystal, too, having the same
(k-1)
-shadow as
W
—namely,
Z
. Similarly,
C
is affine by virtue of Proposition
55
(
v
). Hence, we are left to show that
C
is hollow. To this end, we show that no tuple
\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}
is a tie for
C
. This is proved by induction over the quantity
\ell(\mathbf{b})=|\{i\in[k]:b_{i}>\hat{n}\}|
. For the basis of the induction, suppose that
\ell(\mathbf{b})=0
(which means that
\mathbf{b}\in[\hat{n}]^{k}
).
Observe that the choice of
\mathbf{y}
guarantees that
\set(\mathbf{y})
is disjoint from
\set(\mathbf{d})
for each
\mathbf{d}\in[\hat{n}]^{k}
.
We find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_2106_15_sept}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast W-\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in[\hat{n}]^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{d}}\ast W)(E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{y}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{P}.\ref{prop_basic_stuff_quartzes}\eqref{lem_part_supp_quartzes}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast W-(E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast W)(E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast Q_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{P}.\ref{prop_basic_stuff_quartzes}\eqref{lem_part_isentryone}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast W-E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}0,

which means, in particular, that
\mathbf{b}
is not a tie for
C
.
Before dealing with the inductive step, we establish the following fact:

If
\mathbf{c}\in\supp(C)
and
c_{i}>\hat{n}
for some
i\in[k]
, then
c_{i}=\hat{n}+i
.

(45)

To prove (
45
), observe that
\set(\mathbf{c})\not\subseteq[\hat{n}]
, so
\mathbf{c}\not\in\supp(W)
. Therefore, it follows from (
44
) that
\mathbf{c}\in\supp(Q_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{y}})
for some
\mathbf{d}\in[\hat{n}]^{k}
.
Using Proposition
55
(
i
), we conclude that
c_{i}=y_{i}=\hat{n}+i
, as claimed.

Take now
\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}
with
\ell(\mathbf{b})\geq 1
, and let
j\in[k]
be such that
b_{j}>\hat{n}
. Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
\mathbf{b}
is a tie for
C
; i.e.,
|\mathbf{b}|<k
and
\mathbf{b}\in\supp(C)
. Let
\alpha<\beta\in[k]
be such that
b_{\alpha}=b_{\beta}
. Notice that
b_{\alpha}=b_{\beta}\in[\hat{n}]
as, otherwise, (
45
) would yield
b_{\alpha}=\hat{n}+\alpha\neq\hat{n}+\beta=b_{\beta}
, a contradiction. In particular, this means that
j\not\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
. Define
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha
if
\alpha<j
, and
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha-1
if
\alpha>j
. Similarly, define
\tilde{\beta}=\beta
if
\beta<j
, and
\tilde{\beta}=\beta-1
if
\beta>j
. Consider also the tuple
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k-1}_{\to}
obtained by removing the
j
-th element from
{\langle k\rangle}
, and observe that
b_{i_{\tilde{\alpha}}}=b_{\alpha}
and
b_{i_{\tilde{\beta}}}=b_{\beta}
, so
b_{i_{\tilde{\alpha}}}=b_{i_{\tilde{\beta}}}
. We note that
\tilde{\alpha}\neq\tilde{\beta}
. Indeed,
\tilde{\alpha}=\tilde{\beta}
would imply that
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha
and
\tilde{\beta}=\beta-1
, from which it would follow that
\alpha<j<\beta
and that
\alpha=\beta-1
, a contradiction.
As a consequence,
|\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}|<k-1
. Since
Z
is hollow, it follows that
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}\not\in\supp(Z)
.
For any
a\in[n]
, let
\mathbf{b}^{(a)}
be the tuple in
[n]^{k}
obtained by replacing the
j
-th element of
\mathbf{b}
with
a
. We find

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{a\in[n]}E_{\mathbf{b}^{(a)}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{a\in[\hat{n}]}E_{\mathbf{b}^{(a)}}\ast C+\sum_{a\in[n]\setminus[\hat{n}]}E_{\mathbf{b}^{(a)}}\ast C

(46)

where the second equality follows from the fact that
Z
is the
(k-1)
-shadow of
C
.
If
a\in[\hat{n}]
,
\ell(\mathbf{b}^{(a)})=\ell(\mathbf{b})-1
. Moreover, using that
j\not\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
, we have
b^{(a)}_{\alpha}=b_{\alpha}=b_{\beta}=b^{(a)}_{\beta}
, which means that
|\mathbf{b}^{(a)}|<k
. Using the inductive hypothesis, we deduce that
\mathbf{b}^{(a)}\not\in\supp(C)
, so
E_{\mathbf{b}^{(a)}}\ast C=0
. If
a\in[n]\setminus[\hat{n}]
and
\mathbf{b}^{(a)}\in\supp(C)
, applying (
45
) twice yields
a=\hat{n}+j=b_{j}
, which implies that
\mathbf{b}^{(a)}=\mathbf{b}
. Therefore, it follows from (
5.5
) that
E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast C=0
, thus contradicting our assumptions. This establishes that
C
is hollow (
\spadesuit
5) and concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Using Theorem
5
, we find a hollow affine
(k-1)
-crystal
\hat{C}\in\mathcal{T}^{\frac{k^{2}+k}{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
. Applying Corollary
50
, we find a
k
-crystal
C\in\mathcal{T}^{\frac{k^{2}+k}{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
whose
k
-shadow is
\hat{C}
. The fact that
C
is affine directly follows from Lemma
54
.
∎

6
Fooling the hierarchy

In this section, we translate the hollow-shadowed crystals built in Section
5
back to the algorithmic framework. This results in a proof of Theorem
6
, which establishes that any loopless digraph is accepted by any level of the
\BA
hierarchy applied to AGC,
provided that
the number of colours is large enough. Then, we prove two results on the
\BA
hierarchy (Propositions
10
and
11
, both consequences of more general results on linear minions) that are able to “boost” Theorem
6
by relaxing the requirement on the number of colours. These are the last ingredients needed to establish that the family of shift digraphs introduced in Section
2.3
provides fooling instances for
all
levels of the
\BA
hierarchy applied to AGC for
all
numbers of colours, and to finally validate the proof of Theorem
1
presented in Section
2.3
.

Theorem

(Theorem
6
restated)
.

Let
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathbf{X}
be a loopless digraph. Then
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{(k^{2}+k)/2})=\textsc{Yes}
.

Proof.

We can assume that
V(\mathbf{X})=[q]
for some
q\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover, by possibly adding isolated vertices to
\mathbf{X}
, we can assume that
q>k
.
Set
n=\frac{k^{2}+k}{2}
.
Applying Theorem
4
, we construct an affine
k
-crystal
C\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}(\mathbb{Z})
whose
k
-shadow
S\in\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})
is hollow. We claim that the map

\displaystyle\zeta:V(\mathbf{X})^{k}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}}\ast C

yields a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.

First of all, we need to check that
\zeta(\mathbf{x})\in V(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))={\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}^{(n^{k})}
for each
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
. This easily follows from the facts that
C
has integer entries and

\displaystyle\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}}\ast C\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}}\right)\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}_{\bm{\epsilon}}}\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}1,

where the last equality holds since
C
is affine.

We now check that
\zeta
sends hyperedges of
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to hyperedges of
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Take
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in E(\mathbf{X})
, so that
\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in E(\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. To prove that
\zeta(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in E(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}}(\mathbf{K}_{n}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))
, we need to find some
{\mathbf{q}}\in{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}^{(|E(\mathbf{K}_{n})|)}={\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}^{(n^{2}-n)}
for which
\zeta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})={\mathbf{q}}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
. By Proposition
42
we have that
C
is a
2
-crystal; let
\tilde{S}
be its
2
-shadow.
Consider the tuple
{\bm{\alpha}}
defined by
{\bm{\alpha}}=(1,2)
if
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
{\bm{\alpha}}=(2,1)
if
x_{1}>x_{2}
(notice that
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
as
\mathbf{X}
is loopless).
Observe that
\mathbf{x}_{\bm{\alpha}}\in[q]^{2}_{\to}
and
{\bm{\alpha}}_{\bm{\alpha}}=(1,2)
. We consider the vector
{\mathbf{q}}\in\mathcal{T}^{n^{2}-n}(\mathbb{Z})
whose
\mathbf{a}
-th entry is
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}
for any
\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n})
. Observe that

\displaystyle\tilde{S}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle k\rangle}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle k\rangle}}\right)\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{{\langle k\rangle}}\ast C\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}\ast S,

(47)

where the first and fifth equalities come from the fact that
\tilde{S}
and
S
are the
2
-shadow and the
k
-shadow of
C
, respectively, while the second equality holds since
{\langle k\rangle}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}={\langle 2\rangle}
.
Therefore, for any
a\in[n]
,

\displaystyle E_{(a,a)}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1805_30sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{(a,a)}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}\ast S\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{(a,a)}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\overset{\tiny 2}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle 2\rangle}}\right)\ast S

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}

\displaystyle E_{(a,a)}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\langle 2\rangle}_{\bm{\alpha}}}\ast S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{(a,a)}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\ast S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\bm{\alpha}}=(a,a)\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}0,

(48)

where the fourth equality holds since
{\langle 2\rangle}_{\bm{\alpha}}={\bm{\alpha}}
, and the sixth follows from the fact that
S
is hollow. Hence, we find

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n})}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n})}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1448_30sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{2}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{Pi_epsilon_all_one}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}}\ast\tilde{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\epsilon}}\ast\tilde{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_conservation_sum_crystals}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}1,

whence it follows that
{\mathbf{q}}\in{\mathscr{Z}_{\operatorname{aff}}}^{(n^{2}-n)}
.
Given
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
,
we have

\displaystyle\zeta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{\mathbf{i}}}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny 2}{\ast}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\overset{\tiny 2}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}_{\bm{\alpha}}}\right)\ast C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}_{\bm{\alpha}}}\ast C\right)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}\right).

It follows that, for any
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}\right)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{2}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1448_30sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n})\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2}}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ast\tilde{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in E(\mathbf{K}_{n})\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast{\mathbf{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_calculation_rule_P}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast{\mathbf{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast{\mathbf{q}}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}},

which concludes the proof that
\zeta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})={\mathbf{q}}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
. Hence,
\zeta
is a homomorphism.

To check that
\zeta
is
k
-tensorial, simply notice that, for any
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
,

\displaystyle\zeta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_proj_contr}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}}\right)\ast C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{x}}\ast C\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x}).

(49)

Take now
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}\in[n]^{k}
, and suppose that
\mathbf{a}\not\prec\mathbf{x}
. If we manage to show that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})=0
, we may apply Theorem
2
and conclude that
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{n})=\textsc{Yes}
, as desired.
Choose
u,v\in[k]
for which
a_{u}=a_{v}
and
x_{u}\neq x_{v}
. Using that
q>k
, we find
\mathbf{y}\in[q]^{k}_{\to}
and
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
for which
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}_{\mathbf{i}}
. We obtain

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{y}_{\mathbf{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1933_30sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{q}}_{\mathbf{y}}\ast C\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast S.

(50)

Suppose that
\mathbf{b}\in[n]^{k}
satisfies
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}
. Then,
b_{i_{u}}=a_{u}=a_{v}=b_{i_{v}}
. On the other hand,
y_{i_{u}}=x_{u}\neq x_{v}=y_{i_{v}}
, which implies that
i_{u}\neq i_{v}
. As a consequence,
|\mathbf{b}|<k
. Since
S
is hollow, we deduce that
\mathbf{b}\not\in\supp(S)
. Hence, it follows from (
6
) that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\zeta(\mathbf{x})=0
, as wanted.
∎

Our next goal is to prove Proposition
10
, which states that
\BA^{k}
acceptance is preserved under the line digraph operator introduced in Section
2.3
, at the cost of halving the level.
In fact, we shall prove that result in the more general setting of arbitrary conic minions, as stated in Proposition
57
. We need a property of conic minions from
[
40
, Proposition 38]
, formally stated below in Proposition
56
: Each relaxation hierarchy built on this type of minions only gives a nonzero
weight to those assignments that yield partial homomorphisms. In other words, each such hierarchy enforces consistency.

Proposition 56

(
[
40
]
)
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a conic minion of depth
d
, let
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be digraphs, and let
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
be a
k
-tensorial homomorphism. Take
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
,
\mathbf{a}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{k}
, and
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{2}
. If
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\in E(\mathbf{X})
and
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}\not\in E(\mathbf{A})
, then
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{0}_{d}
.

Proposition 57
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a conic minion, let
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be
digraphs, and suppose that there exists a
(2k)
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}})
.
Then there exists a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
(\delta\mathbf{X})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}((\delta\mathbf{A})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.

Proof.

As usual, we let
n=|V(\mathbf{A})|
; moreover, we let
m=|E(\mathbf{A})|
.
Take a
(2k)
-tensorial homomorphism
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}})
,
whose existence is guaranteed by the hypothesis.

Suppose first that
E(\delta\mathbf{A})=\emptyset
. We claim that, in this case,
E(\delta\mathbf{X})=\emptyset
. Otherwise, take some element
((x,y),(y,z))\in E(\delta\mathbf{X})
, and consider a tuple
\mathbf{w}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{2k}
satisfying
\mathbf{w}_{{\langle 3\rangle}}=(x,y,z)
(where we have used that
k\geq 2
). Since the minion
{\mathscr{M}}
is conic,
\xi(\mathbf{w})
is not all zero. Hence, there exists some
\mathbf{a}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}
such that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\neq\mathbf{0}_{d}
, where
d
is the depth of
{\mathscr{M}}
. Applying Proposition
56
to the cases
\mathbf{i}=(1,2)
and
\mathbf{i}=(2,3)
, we deduce that
\mathbf{a}_{(1,2)}\in E(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathbf{a}_{(2,3)}\in E(\mathbf{A})
. But this means that
((a_{1},a_{2}),(a_{2},a_{3}))\in E(\delta\mathbf{A})
, a contradiction. Hence, as claimed,
E(\delta\mathbf{X})=\emptyset
. As a consequence, any map from
E(\mathbf{X})
to
E(\mathbf{A})
yields a homomorphism
\delta\mathbf{X}\to\delta\mathbf{A}
. Thus, it follows from the completeness of minion tests (see
[
40
, Proposition 13]
) that a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
(\delta\mathbf{X})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}((\delta\mathbf{A})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
exists for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Suppose now that
E(\delta\mathbf{A})\neq\emptyset
.
Fix
\textbf{t}=(\textbf{e},\textbf{f})\in E(\delta\mathbf{A})
, where
\textbf{e},\textbf{f}\in E(\mathbf{A})
, and consider the maps

\displaystyle\alpha:V(\mathbf{A})^{2}

\displaystyle\to E(\mathbf{A}),

\displaystyle\beta:V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}

\displaystyle\to E(\mathbf{A})^{k}

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(a,b)&\mbox{ if }(a,b)\in E(\mathbf{A})\\
\textbf{e}&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\alpha(\mathbf{a}_{(1,2)}),\alpha(\mathbf{a}_{(3,4)}),\dots,\alpha(\mathbf{a}_{(2k-1,2k)})).

Consider also the map
\gamma:E(\mathbf{X})^{k}\to V(\mathbf{X})^{2k}
sending a tuple
(\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(k)})
of edges of
\mathbf{X}
to the tuple
(x^{(1)}_{1},x^{(1)}_{2},x^{(2)}_{1},x^{(2)}_{2},\dots,x^{(k)}_{1},x^{(k)}_{2})
of vertices of
\mathbf{X}
, where
\mathbf{x}^{(i)}=(x^{(i)}_{1},x^{(i)}_{2})
for each
i\in[k]
.
We define the map
\vartheta:E(\mathbf{X})^{k}\to{\mathscr{M}}^{(m^{k})}
by setting
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{x}))_{/\beta}
for each
\mathbf{x}\in E(\mathbf{X})^{k}
.
(Observe that the definition of
\vartheta
is independent of the choice of
\mathbf{e}\in E(\mathbf{A})
, because of Proposition
56
.)
The result would follow if we prove that
\vartheta
yields a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
(\delta\mathbf{X})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}((\delta\mathbf{A})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.
Observe first that
V((\delta\mathbf{X})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})=V(\delta\mathbf{X})^{k}=E(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
V(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}((\delta\mathbf{A})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))={\mathscr{M}}^{(|V((\delta\mathbf{A})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})|)}={\mathscr{M}}^{(|V(\delta\mathbf{A})^{k}|)}={\mathscr{M}}^{(|E(\mathbf{A})^{k}|)}={\mathscr{M}}^{(m^{k})}
, so the domain and codomain of
\vartheta
are correct.
Take
\mathbf{v}=((x,y),(y,z))\in E(\delta\mathbf{X})
(so both
(x,y)
and
(y,z)
belong to
E(\mathbf{X})
) and consider the tensor
\mathbf{v}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in E((\delta\mathbf{X})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. To conclude that
\vartheta
is a homomorphism, we need to show that
\vartheta(\mathbf{v}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in E(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}((\delta\mathbf{A})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))
; i.e., we need to find some
Q\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(|E(\delta\mathbf{A})|)}
such that
\vartheta(\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}})=Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
, where
\pi_{\mathbf{i}}:E(\delta\mathbf{A})\to V(\delta\mathbf{A})^{k}=E(\mathbf{A})^{k}
is the map sending
\mathbf{d}\in E(\delta\mathbf{A})
to
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Using that
k\geq 2
, we can consider a tuple
\mathbf{w}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{2k}
satisfying
\mathbf{w}_{{\langle 3\rangle}}=(x,y,z)
.
Consider the set
S=\{\mathbf{a}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}:\mathbf{a}_{(\ell,\ell+1)}\in E(\mathbf{A})\mbox{ for }\ell\in[2]\}
. It follows directly from Proposition
56
that

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{a}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}:E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\neq\mathbf{0}_{d}\}\;\;\subseteq\;\;S.

(51)

Take the function

\displaystyle\tau:V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}

\displaystyle\to E(\delta\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(\mathbf{a}_{(1,2)},\mathbf{a}_{(2,3)})&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}\in S\\
\textbf{t}&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

We define
Q=\xi(\mathbf{w})_{/\tau}
. (Note that
Q
does not depend on the choice of
t
, because of Proposition
56
.) Let
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
; we need to show that
\vartheta(\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}})=Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
. Consider the tuple
\mathbf{j}\in[3]^{2k}
defined by
j_{2\ell-1}=i_{\ell}
,
j_{2\ell}=i_{\ell}+1
for each
\ell\in[k]
, and notice that
\gamma(\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}})=\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{j}}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\vartheta(\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}}))_{/\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\xi(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{j}})_{/\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{w}_{\mathbf{j}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{w})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{w}),

(52)

where the fourth equality follows from the fact that
\xi
is
(2k)
-tensorial,
while

\displaystyle Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(\xi(\mathbf{w})_{/\tau})_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_composition_minors}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\xi(\mathbf{w})_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{w}).

(53)

Consider the function
\rho:V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}\to V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}
defined by
\mathbf{c}\mapsto\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}}
for each
\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}
.
Observe that the functions
\beta\circ\rho
and
\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau
coincide on the set
S\subseteq V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}
. Indeed, for any
\mathbf{c}\in S
,

\displaystyle\beta\circ\rho(\mathbf{c})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\beta(\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\beta((c_{i_{1}},c_{i_{1}+1},c_{i_{2}},c_{i_{2}+1},\dots,c_{i_{k}},c_{i_{k}+1}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}((c_{i_{1}},c_{i_{1}+1}),(c_{i_{2}},c_{i_{2}+1}),\dots,(c_{i_{k}},c_{i_{k}+1}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(\mathbf{c}_{(1,2)},\mathbf{c}_{(2,3)})_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(\tau(\mathbf{c}))_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau(\mathbf{c}).

(54)

For
\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{A})^{k}
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_26sep_1151_a}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\left(P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{w})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\beta}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_calculation_rule_P}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}\\
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}\\
\beta(\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}})=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_halloween_22_32}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in S\\
\beta(\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}})=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in S\\
\beta\circ\rho(\mathbf{c})=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_1237_26sep}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in S\\
\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau(\mathbf{c})=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_halloween_22_32}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}\\
\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau(\mathbf{c})=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_calculation_rule_P}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau}\ast\xi(\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\left(P_{\pi_{\mathbf{i}}\circ\tau}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{w})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_26sep_1151_b}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}},

which concludes the proof that
\vartheta(\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}})=Q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
, thus establishing that
\vartheta
is a homomorphism.

We are left to prove that
\vartheta
is
k
-tensorial. To that end, consider some tuples
\mathbf{x}\in E(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
. We need to show that
\vartheta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\vartheta(\mathbf{x})
. Consider the tuple
\mathbf{j}\in[2k]^{2k}
defined by
j_{2\ell-1}=2i_{\ell}-1
,
j_{2\ell}=2i_{\ell}
for each
\ell\in[k]
, and observe that
\gamma(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=(\gamma(\mathbf{x}))_{\mathbf{j}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\vartheta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}))_{/\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\xi((\gamma(\mathbf{x}))_{\mathbf{j}})_{/\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi((\gamma(\mathbf{x}))_{\mathbf{j}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{x}))\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{x})),

where the fourth equality follows from the fact that
\xi
is
(2k)
-tensorial. Moreover,

\displaystyle\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\vartheta(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{x}))_{/\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\left(P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{x}))\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\xi(\gamma(\mathbf{x})).

The claim would then follow if we show that the two tensors
P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}
and
\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{\beta}
coincide. To that end, observe first that the identity
\beta(\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}})=(\beta(\mathbf{c}))_{\mathbf{i}}
holds for any
\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}
. Hence, for each
\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{A})^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\left(P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\beta}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_calculation_rule_P}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}\\
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}\\
\beta(\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}})=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{2k}\\
(\beta(\mathbf{c}))_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in E(\mathbf{A})^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\beta^{-1}(\mathbf{b})}E_{\mathbf{c}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_calculation_rule_P}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in E(\mathbf{A})^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast P_{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast P_{\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\left(\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{\beta}\right).

It follows that
P_{\beta}\overset{\tiny 2k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{2k}}_{\mathbf{j}}=\Pi^{m\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{\beta}
,
as desired.
∎

Proposition

(Proposition
10
restated)
.

Let
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be digraphs, and suppose that
\BA^{2k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})=\textsc{Yes}
.
Then
\BA^{k}(\delta\mathbf{X},\delta\mathbf{A})=\textsc{Yes}
.

Proof.

The result immediately follows from Proposition
57
and Theorem
32
and from the fact that
{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}
is a conic minion (cf. Example
27
).
∎

We next show that acceptance of hierarchies of relaxations built on linear minions is preserved under homomorphisms of the template. Proposition
11
—the last missing piece in the proof of Theorem
1
—will then follow as a corollary.

Proposition 58
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}
be a linear minion, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
be digraphs such that
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, and suppose that there exists a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Then there exists a
k
-tensorial homomorphism
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.

Proof.

Let
f:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
be a homomorphism, and consider the functions
g:V(\mathbf{A})^{k}\to V(\mathbf{B})^{k}
defined by
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\mapsto(f(a_{1}),\dots,f(a_{k}))
and
h:E(\mathbf{A})\to E(\mathbf{B})
defined by
(a_{1},a_{2})\mapsto(f(a_{1}),f(a_{2}))
. (Notice that
h
is well defined as
f
is a homomorphism.)
Suppose, without loss of generality, that
V(\mathbf{A})=[n]
and
V(\mathbf{B})=[p]
for some
n,p\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\xi
be a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
, and consider the function

\displaystyle\vartheta:V(\mathbf{X})^{k}

\displaystyle\to{\mathscr{M}}^{(p^{k})}.

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\xi(\mathbf{x})_{/g}

We claim that
\vartheta
is a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.

To show that
\vartheta
is a homomorphism, take
\mathbf{x}\in E(\mathbf{X})
, so
\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in E(\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Since
\xi
is a homomorphism,
\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in E(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))
, so there exists
Q\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(|E(\mathbf{A})|)}
such that
\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=Q_{/\pi^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
—where the superscript “
\mathbf{A}
” indicates that
\pi_{\mathbf{i}}
is defined for the digraph
\mathbf{A}
; i.e.,
\pi^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{i}}:E(\mathbf{A})\to V(\mathbf{A})^{k}
is the function given by
\mathbf{a}\mapsto\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Define
W=Q_{/h}\in{\mathscr{M}}^{(|E(\mathbf{B})|)}
. Given
\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
, let
\pi^{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathbf{i}}:E(\mathbf{B})\to V(\mathbf{B})^{k}
be the function given by
\mathbf{b}\mapsto\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Note that

g\circ\pi^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{i}}=\pi^{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathbf{i}}\circ h
.
Indeed, for any
\mathbf{a}\in E(\mathbf{A})
, we have

\displaystyle g(\pi^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{i}}(\mathbf{a}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}g(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(f(a_{i_{1}}),\dots,f(a_{i_{k}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(f(a_{1}),f(a_{2}))_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(h(\mathbf{a}))_{\mathbf{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\pi^{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathbf{i}}(h(\mathbf{a})).

Therefore, we find

\displaystyle\vartheta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})_{/g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(Q_{/\pi^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{i}}})_{/g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_composition_minors}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}Q_{/g\circ\pi^{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathbf{i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}Q_{/\pi^{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathbf{i}}\circ h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqn_composition_minors}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}(Q_{/h})_{/\pi^{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathbf{i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}W_{/\pi^{\mathbf{B}}_{\mathbf{i}}}.

It follows that
\vartheta(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in E(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}}}(\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}))
, so
\vartheta
is a homomorphism.

To show that
\vartheta
is
k
-tensorial, take
\mathbf{x}\in V(\mathbf{X})^{k}
and
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
.
Using that
\xi
is
k
-tensorial, we find

\displaystyle\vartheta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})_{/g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{x})\right)_{/g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{g}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\left(\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{x})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}P_{g}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{x}),

while

\displaystyle\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\vartheta(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{x})_{/g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\left(P_{g}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{x})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{g}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\xi(\mathbf{x}).

Therefore, to obtain
\vartheta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\vartheta(\mathbf{x})
and thus conclude that
\vartheta
is
k
-tensorial, it suffices to prove that
P_{g}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}=\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{g}
.
To that end, we apply a similar argument to the one used at the end of the proof of Proposition
57
.
Notice that both these tensors belong to
\mathcal{T}^{(p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k},n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k})}(\mathbb{Q})
. Given
\mathbf{a}\in V(\mathbf{A})^{k}
and
\mathbf{b}\in V(\mathbf{B})^{k}
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\left(P_{g}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\right)\ast E_{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast P_{g}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast E_{\mathbf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_calculation_rule_P}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in g^{-1}(\mathbf{b})}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast E_{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{ if }\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}\in g^{-1}(\mathbf{b})\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{ if }g(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}})=\mathbf{b}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise},\end{array}\right.

while

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\left(\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{g}\right)\ast E_{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_associativity_contraction}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\ast P_{g}\ast E_{\mathbf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{L}.\ref{lem_Pi_basic}}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{d}\in V(\mathbf{B})^{k}\\
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{d}}\ast P_{g}\ast E_{\mathbf{a}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{ if }(g(\mathbf{a}))_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{b}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Since
g(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}})=(g(\mathbf{a}))_{\mathbf{i}}
, the two expressions above coincide, thus implying that
P_{g}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}\Pi^{n\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}=\Pi^{p\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}}_{\mathbf{i}}\overset{\tiny k}{\ast}P_{g}
, as required.
∎

Proposition

(Proposition
11
restated)
.

Let
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
be digraphs such that
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, and suppose that
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})=\textsc{Yes}
. Then
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{B})=\textsc{Yes}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
32
,
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})=\textsc{Yes}
implies the existence of a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. By Proposition
58
, it follows that there exists a
k
-tensorial homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{{\mathscr{M}_{\operatorname{\BA}}}}(\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Again by Theorem
32
, we conclude that
\BA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{B})=\textsc{Yes}
.
∎
